### Q61. MHT CET 2025 (26 Apr Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

A capillary tube when immersed vertically in water, the rise of water column is upto height $h_1$ on earth's surface. When this arrangement is taken into a mine of depth ' d ', below earth's surface, the height of the water column is $h_2$. If $R$ is the radius of the earth, the ratio $\frac{h_2}{h_1}$ is

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\frac{\mathrm{R}+\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{R}}$
- **(B)** $\frac{\mathrm{R}-\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{R}}$
- **(C)** $\frac{R}{R+d}$
- **(D)** $\frac{\mathrm{R}}{\mathrm{R}-\mathrm{d}}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(D) $\frac{\mathrm{R}}{\mathrm{R}-\mathrm{d}}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The height of capillary rise is given by Jurin's law:

$h = \dfrac{2T \cos \theta}{r \rho g}$

Since $T$, $\theta$, $r$, and $\rho$ remain constant, the height $h$ is inversely proportional to the acceleration due to gravity $g$.

$h \propto \dfrac{1}{g}$

On the surface of the earth, the height is $h_1 \propto \dfrac{1}{g}$.

At a depth $d$ below the earth's surface, the acceleration due to gravity is:

$g_d = g \left(1 - \dfrac{d}{R}\right) = g \left(\dfrac{R-d}{R}\right)$

The height of capillary rise at depth $d$ is $h_2 \propto \dfrac{1}{g_d}$.

Taking the ratio of $h_2$ to $h_1$:

$\dfrac{h_2}{h_1} = \dfrac{g}{g_d} = \dfrac{g}{g \left(\dfrac{R-d}{R}\right)} = \dfrac{R}{R-d}$

Answer: $\frac{\mathrm{R}}{\mathrm{R}-\mathrm{d}}$

</details>

---

### Q62. MHT CET 2025 (25 Apr Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

When one end of a capillary tube is dipped in water, the height of water column is ' $h$ '. The upward force of 105 dyne due to surface tension is balanced by the force due to the weight of water column. The inner circumference of the capillary tube is
(Surface tension of water $=7 \times 10^{-2} \mathrm{~N} / \mathrm{m}$ )

**Options:**

- **(A)** $1.5 \mathrm{~cm}$
- **(B)** 2 cm
- **(C)** $2.5 \mathrm{~cm}$
- **(D)** 3 cm

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) $1.5 \mathrm{~cm}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The upward force due to surface tension is given by $F = T \times C \times \cos \theta$, where $C$ is the inner circumference of the capillary tube.

For water, the angle of contact $\theta = 0^\circ$, so $\cos \theta = 1$.

Given $F = 105 \text{ dyne} = 105 \times 10^{-5} \text{ N}$ and $T = 7 \times 10^{-2} \text{ N/m}$.

Substituting the values:

$105 \times 10^{-5} = 7 \times 10^{-2} \times C$

$C = \dfrac{105 \times 10^{-5}}{7 \times 10^{-2}}$

$C = 15 \times 10^{-3} \text{ m}$

$C = 1.5 \times 10^{-2} \text{ m} = 1.5 \text{ cm}$

Answer: $1.5 \mathrm{~cm}$

</details>

---

### Q63. MHT CET 2025 (25 Apr Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

The amount of work done in blowing a soap bubble such that its diameter increases from ' d ' to ' D ' is ( $\mathrm{T}=$ surface tension of solution)

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\pi\left(D^2-d^2\right) T$
- **(B)** $2 \pi\left(D^2-d^2\right) T$
- **(C)** $4 \pi\left(D^2-d^2\right) T$
- **(D)** $8 \pi\left(D^2-d^2\right) T$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) $2 \pi\left(D^2-d^2\right) T$**

**Detailed Solution:**

A soap bubble has two free surfaces, one inside and one outside.

Initial radius of the bubble, $r_1 = \dfrac{d}{2}$

Final radius of the bubble, $r_2 = \dfrac{D}{2}$

Initial total surface area, $A_1 = 2 \times 4 \pi r_1^2 = 8 \pi \left(\dfrac{d}{2}\right)^2 = 2 \pi d^2$

Final total surface area, $A_2 = 2 \times 4 \pi r_2^2 = 8 \pi \left(\dfrac{D}{2}\right)^2 = 2 \pi D^2$

Increase in surface area, $\Delta A = A_2 - A_1 = 2 \pi (D^2 - d^2)$

Work done in blowing the bubble is given by the product of surface tension and the increase in surface area:

$W = T \times \Delta A = 2 \pi (D^2 - d^2) T$

Answer: $2 \pi\left(D^2-d^2\right) T$

</details>

---

### Q64. MHT CET 2025 (25 Apr Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

In a capillary tube of area of cross-section 'a' water rises to height 'h'. To what height will water rise in a capillary tube of area of cross-section $4 a$ ?

**Options:**

- **(A)** $4 h$
- **(B)** $2 h$
- **(C)** $\frac{\mathrm{h}}{2}$
- **(D)** $\frac{\mathrm{h}}{4}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) $\frac{\mathrm{h}}{2}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The height of capillary rise is given by $h = \dfrac{2T \cos \theta}{r \rho g}$

This implies $h \propto \dfrac{1}{r}$

The area of cross-section of the capillary tube is $a = \pi r^2$, which gives $r \propto \sqrt{a}$

Therefore, $h \propto \dfrac{1}{\sqrt{a}}$

For the two capillary tubes, we can write $\dfrac{h_2}{h_1} = \sqrt{\dfrac{a_1}{a_2}}$

Given $a_1 = a$ and $a_2 = 4a$, we get $\dfrac{h_2}{h} = \sqrt{\dfrac{a}{4a}} = \dfrac{1}{2}$

$h_2 = \dfrac{h}{2}$

Answer: $\frac{\mathrm{h}}{2}$

</details>

---

### Q65. MHT CET 2025 (23 Apr Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

The energy needed for breaking a liquid drop of radius ' $R$ ' into 216 droplets, each of radius ' $r$ ' is ' $x$ ' times TR ${ }^2$. The value of ' $x$ ' is [ $\mathrm{T}=$ surface tension of the liquid]

**Options:**

- **(A)** $4 \pi$
- **(B)** $12 \pi$
- **(C)** $180 \pi$
- **(D)** $20 \pi$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(D) $20 \pi$**

**Detailed Solution:**

By the conservation of volume, the volume of the initial drop is equal to the total volume of the 216 droplets.

$\dfrac{4}{3} \pi R^3 = 216 \times \dfrac{4}{3} \pi r^3$

$R^3 = 216 r^3 \Rightarrow R = 6r \Rightarrow r = \dfrac{R}{6}$

The initial surface area of the drop is:

$A_1 = 4 \pi R^2$

The final surface area of the 216 droplets is:

$A_2 = 216 \times 4 \pi r^2$

Substituting $r = \dfrac{R}{6}$:

$A_2 = 216 \times 4 \pi \left(\dfrac{R}{6}\right)^2 = 216 \times 4 \pi \left(\dfrac{R^2}{36}\right) = 24 \pi R^2$

The increase in surface area is:

$\Delta A = A_2 - A_1 = 24 \pi R^2 - 4 \pi R^2 = 20 \pi R^2$

The energy required to break the drop is given by the product of surface tension and the increase in surface area:

$E = T \Delta A = T(20 \pi R^2) = 20 \pi T R^2$

Comparing this with the given expression $x T R^2$, we get:

$x = 20 \pi$

Answer: $20 \pi$

</details>

---

### Q66. MHT CET 2025 (23 Apr Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

The amount of work done in blowing a soap bubble such that its diameter increases from $d_1$ to $d_2$ is ( $T=$ surface tension of soap solution)

**Options:**

- **(A)** $4 \pi\left(\mathrm{~d}_2^2-\mathrm{d}_1^2\right) \mathrm{T}$
- **(B)** $8 \pi\left(\mathrm{~d}_2^2-\mathrm{d}_1^2\right) \mathrm{T}$
- **(C)** $\pi\left(\mathrm{d}_2^2-\mathrm{d}_1^2\right) \mathrm{T}$
- **(D)** $2 \pi\left(\mathrm{~d}_2^2-\mathrm{d}_1^2\right) \mathrm{T}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(D) $2 \pi\left(\mathrm{~d}_2^2-\mathrm{d}_1^2\right) \mathrm{T}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

A soap bubble has two free surfaces (inner and outer).

Initial surface area of the soap bubble, $A_1 = 2 \times 4 \pi r_1^2 = 2 \times 4 \pi \left(\dfrac{d_1}{2}\right)^2 = 2 \pi d_1^2$

Final surface area of the soap bubble, $A_2 = 2 \times 4 \pi r_2^2 = 2 \times 4 \pi \left(\dfrac{d_2}{2}\right)^2 = 2 \pi d_2^2$

Increase in surface area, $\Delta A = A_2 - A_1 = 2 \pi (d_2^2 - d_1^2)$

Work done in blowing the bubble is given by the product of surface tension and the increase in surface area:

$W = T \times \Delta A = 2 \pi (d_2^2 - d_1^2) T$

Answer: $2 \pi\left(\mathrm{~d}_2^2-\mathrm{d}_1^2\right) \mathrm{T}$

</details>

---

### Q67. MHT CET 2025 (23 Apr Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

A disc of paper of radius ' $R$ ' has a hole of radius ' $r$ '. It is floating on a liquid of surface tension ' T '. The force of surface tension on the disc is

**Options:**

- **(A)** $2 \pi \mathrm{~T}(\mathrm{R}-\mathrm{r})$
- **(B)** $2 \pi \mathrm{~T}(\mathrm{R}+\mathrm{r})$
- **(C)** $3 \pi \mathrm{~T} \cdot \mathrm{R}$
- **(D)** $4 \pi \mathrm{~T}(\mathrm{R}+\mathrm{r})$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) $2 \pi \mathrm{~T}(\mathrm{R}+\mathrm{r})$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The force of surface tension acts on the total boundary of the paper disc in contact with the liquid.

The disc has two boundaries: the outer edge of radius $R$ and the inner edge of the hole of radius $r$.

Length of the outer boundary $= 2\pi R$

Length of the inner boundary $= 2\pi r$

Total length of the boundary in contact with the liquid is $L = 2\pi R + 2\pi r = 2\pi(R + r)$.

The force of surface tension is given by $F = T \times L$.

Substituting the value of $L$, we get $F = T \times 2\pi(R + r) = 2\pi T(R + r)$.

Answer: $2 \pi \mathrm{~T}(\mathrm{R}+\mathrm{r})$

</details>

---

### Q68. MHT CET 2025 (23 Apr Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

The excess pressure inside first soap bubble is three times that of a second soap bubble. The ratio of volumes of the first to second bubble

**Options:**

- **(A)** $1: 3$
- **(B)** $1: 9$
- **(C)** $1: 27$
- **(D)** $27: 1$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) $1: 27$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The excess pressure inside a soap bubble is given by $P = \dfrac{4T}{R}$

Given $P_1 = 3P_2$

$\dfrac{4T}{R_1} = 3 \left(\dfrac{4T}{R_2}\right)$

$\dfrac{R_1}{R_2} = \dfrac{1}{3}$

The volume of a spherical bubble is $V = \dfrac{4}{3}\pi R^3$

The ratio of their volumes is:

$\dfrac{V_1}{V_2} = \left(\dfrac{R_1}{R_2}\right)^3 = \left(\dfrac{1}{3}\right)^3 = \dfrac{1}{27}$

Answer: $1: 27$

</details>

### Q69. MHT CET 2025 (22 Apr Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

Water rises in a capillary tube of radius $r$ upto a height $h$. The mass of water in a capillary is $m$. The mass of water that will rise in capillary of radius $\frac{\mathrm{r}}{5}$ will be

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\frac{\mathrm{m}}{5}$
- **(B)** $\frac{\mathrm{m}}{2}$
- **(C)** m
- **(D)** $\frac{\mathrm{m}}{25}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) $\frac{\mathrm{m}}{5}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The height of liquid in a capillary tube is given by Jurin's law:

$h = \dfrac{2T \cos \theta}{r \rho g}$

The mass of the liquid in the capillary tube is:

$m = \pi r^2 h \rho$

Substituting the expression for $h$ into the mass equation:

$m = \pi r^2 \left( \dfrac{2T \cos \theta}{r \rho g} \right) \rho = \dfrac{2 \pi r T \cos \theta}{g}$

This shows that the mass of the liquid raised is directly proportional to the radius of the capillary tube, i.e., $m \propto r$.

For the new capillary tube, the radius is $r' = \dfrac{r}{5}$.

Therefore, the new mass $m'$ will be:

$m' = \dfrac{m}{5}$

Answer: $\frac{\mathrm{m}}{5}$

</details>

---

### Q70. MHT CET 2025 (22 Apr Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

The excess pressure inside a soap bubble is 1.5 times the excess pressure inside a second soap bubble. The volume of the second bubble is ' $x$ ' times the volume of the first bubble. The value of ' $x$ ' is

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\frac{3}{2}$
- **(B)** $\frac{9}{4}$
- **(C)** $\frac{8}{27}$
- **(D)** $\frac{27}{8}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(D) $\frac{27}{8}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The excess pressure inside a soap bubble of radius $R$ and surface tension $T$ is given by $P = \dfrac{4T}{R}$.

Let the excess pressures of the first and second bubbles be $P_1$ and $P_2$, and their radii be $R_1$ and $R_2$ respectively.

Given that $P_1 = 1.5 P_2 = \dfrac{3}{2} P_2$.

$\dfrac{4T}{R_1} = \dfrac{3}{2} \left(\dfrac{4T}{R_2}\right)$

$\dfrac{R_2}{R_1} = \dfrac{3}{2}$

The volume of a soap bubble is $V = \dfrac{4}{3}\pi R^3$.

The ratio of the volume of the second bubble to the first bubble is:

$x = \dfrac{V_2}{V_1} = \left(\dfrac{R_2}{R_1}\right)^3$

$x = \left(\dfrac{3}{2}\right)^3 = \dfrac{27}{8}$

Answer: $\frac{27}{8}$

</details>

### Q71. MHT CET 2025 (22 Apr Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

The energy needed for breaking a liquid drop of radius ' R ' into ' n ' droplets each of radius ' $r$ ' is
$\text { [ } \mathrm{T}=\text { surface tension of the liquid] }$

**Options:**

- **(A)** $4 \pi \mathrm{~T} \mathrm{R}^2\left[\frac{\mathrm{R}}{\mathrm{r}}+1\right]$
- **(B)** $4 \pi \mathrm{~T} \mathrm{R}^2\left[\frac{\mathrm{R}}{\mathrm{r}}-1\right]$
- **(C)** $4 \pi \mathrm{~T} \mathrm{R}^2\left[\frac{\mathrm{r}}{\mathrm{R}}+1\right]$
- **(D)** $4 \pi \mathrm{Tr}^2\left[\frac{\mathrm{R}}{\mathrm{r}}-1\right]$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) $4 \pi \mathrm{~T} \mathrm{R}^2\left[\frac{\mathrm{R}}{\mathrm{r}}-1\right]$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Volume of the initial drop is equal to the total volume of $n$ droplets.

$\dfrac{4}{3} \pi R^3 = n \times \dfrac{4}{3} \pi r^3$

$n = \dfrac{R^3}{r^3}$

Initial surface area of the drop, $A_1 = 4 \pi R^2$

Final surface area of $n$ droplets, $A_2 = n \times 4 \pi r^2$

Change in surface area, $\Delta A = A_2 - A_1 = n \times 4 \pi r^2 - 4 \pi R^2$

Substituting $n = \dfrac{R^3}{r^3}$:

$\Delta A = \left(\dfrac{R^3}{r^3}\right) 4 \pi r^2 - 4 \pi R^2$

$\Delta A = 4 \pi \dfrac{R^3}{r} - 4 \pi R^2$

$\Delta A = 4 \pi R^2 \left[\dfrac{R}{r} - 1\right]$

Energy needed to break the drop is given by $E = T \Delta A$

$E = 4 \pi T R^2 \left[\dfrac{R}{r} - 1\right]$

Answer: $4 \pi \mathrm{~T} \mathrm{R}^2\left[\frac{\mathrm{R}}{\mathrm{r}}-1\right]$

</details>

---

### Q72. MHT CET 2025 (22 Apr Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

A capillary tube is taken from earth's surface to moon's surface. The rise of liquid column on the moon's surface is (acceleration due to gravity on the earth's surface is six times that of moon's surface)

**Options:**

- **(A)** zero.
- **(B)** six times that on the earth's surface.
- **(C)** equal to that on the earth's surface.
- **(D)** $\left(\frac{1}{6}\right)^{\text {th }}$ that on the earth's surface.

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) six times that on the earth's surface.**

**Detailed Solution:**

The formula for the rise of a liquid column in a capillary tube is given by

$h = \dfrac{2T \cos \theta}{r \rho g}$

For a given capillary tube and liquid, $T$, $\theta$, $r$, and $\rho$ are constant. Thus, the capillary rise is inversely proportional to the acceleration due to gravity.

$h \propto \dfrac{1}{g}$

$\dfrac{h_m}{h_e} = \dfrac{g_e}{g_m}$

Given that the acceleration due to gravity on the earth's surface is six times that on the moon's surface, $g_e = 6g_m$.

$\dfrac{h_m}{h_e} = \dfrac{6g_m}{g_m} = 6$

$h_m = 6h_e$

The rise of the liquid column on the moon's surface is six times that on the earth's surface.

Answer: six times that on the earth's surface.

</details>

### Q73. MHT CET 2025 (22 Apr Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

A rectangular film of liquid is expanded from ( $5 \mathrm{~cm} \times 4 \mathrm{~cm}$ ) to ( $7 \mathrm{~cm} \times 8 \mathrm{~cm}$ ). If the work done is $3 \times 10^{-4} \mathrm{~J}$, the surface tension of the liquid is (nearly)

**Options:**

- **(A)** $0.4 \mathrm{~N} / \mathrm{m}$
- **(B)** $0.04 \mathrm{~N} / \mathrm{m}$
- **(C)** 0.4 dyne/cm
- **(D)** $4.0 \mathrm{~N} / \mathrm{m}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) $0.04 \mathrm{~N} / \mathrm{m}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Initial area of the film, $A_1 = 5 \text{ cm} \times 4 \text{ cm} = 20 \text{ cm}^2 = 20 \times 10^{-4} \text{ m}^2$

Final area of the film, $A_2 = 7 \text{ cm} \times 8 \text{ cm} = 56 \text{ cm}^2 = 56 \times 10^{-4} \text{ m}^2$

Increase in area of the film, $\Delta A = A_2 - A_1 = 36 \times 10^{-4} \text{ m}^2$

Since a liquid film has two free surfaces, the total increase in surface area is $2 \times \Delta A = 2 \times 36 \times 10^{-4} \text{ m}^2 = 72 \times 10^{-4} \text{ m}^2$

Work done, $W = T \times 2 \Delta A$

$3 \times 10^{-4} = T \times 72 \times 10^{-4}$

$T = \dfrac{3}{72} = \dfrac{1}{24} \approx 0.0416 \text{ N/m}$

The closest value is $0.04 \text{ N/m}$.

Answer: $0.04 \mathrm{~N} / \mathrm{m}$

</details>

---

### Q74. MHT CET 2025 (21 Apr Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

Two capillary tubes of same diameter are kept vertically in two liquids whose densities are in the ratio $4: 3$. If their surface tensions are in the ratio 6:5, the ratio of heights $\left(\frac{h_1}{h_2}\right)$ of liquids in the two capillary tubes is (Their angle of contacts are same)

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\frac{10}{7}$
- **(B)** $\frac{9}{10}$
- **(C)** $\frac{10}{9}$
- **(D)** $\frac{1}{2}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) $\frac{9}{10}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The height of a liquid in a capillary tube is given by the formula:

$h = \dfrac{2T \cos\theta}{r \rho g}$

Since the capillary tubes have the same diameter, $r_1 = r_2$. The angle of contact is also the same, so $\theta_1 = \theta_2$. Therefore, the height $h$ is directly proportional to the surface tension $T$ and inversely proportional to the density $\rho$:

$h \propto \dfrac{T}{\rho}$

The ratio of the heights of the liquids in the two capillary tubes is:

$\dfrac{h_1}{h_2} = \dfrac{T_1}{T_2} \times \dfrac{\rho_2}{\rho_1}$

Given that $\dfrac{T_1}{T_2} = \dfrac{6}{5}$ and $\dfrac{\rho_1}{\rho_2} = \dfrac{4}{3}$, we get:

$\dfrac{h_1}{h_2} = \dfrac{6}{5} \times \dfrac{3}{4}$

$\dfrac{h_1}{h_2} = \dfrac{18}{20} = \dfrac{9}{10}$

Answer: $\frac{9}{10}$

</details>

---

### Q75. MHT CET 2025 (21 Apr Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

In air, a soap bubble of radius $R$ breaks into 64 small soap bubbles of equal radius $r$. Assuming the surface tension remains unchanged, the ratio of excess pressure inside the big soap bubble to that inside a small soap bubble is

**Options:**

- **(A)** $1: 4$
- **(B)** $4: 1$
- **(C)** $2: 1$
- **(D)** $1: 2$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) $1: 4$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The volume of air is conserved when the bubble breaks up.

$\dfrac{4}{3}\pi R^3 = 64 \times \dfrac{4}{3}\pi r^3 \Rightarrow R^3 = 64 r^3 \Rightarrow R = 4r$

A soap bubble has two free surfaces, so the excess pressure inside a bubble of radius $R$ is $\Delta P = \dfrac{4T}{R}$.

$\dfrac{\Delta P_{\text{big}}}{\Delta P_{\text{small}}} = \dfrac{4T/R}{4T/r} = \dfrac{r}{R} = \dfrac{r}{4r} = \dfrac{1}{4}$

Therefore, the required ratio is $1 : 4$.

</details>

---

### Q76. MHT CET 2025 (21 Apr Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

Water rises up to height ' $x$ ' in a capillary tube immersed vertically in water. When the whole arrangement is taken to a depth ' d ' in a mine, the water level rises up to height Y . If R is the radius of the earth then the ratio $\mathrm{Y}: \mathrm{x}$ is

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\mathrm{R}:(\mathrm{R}+\mathrm{d})$
- **(B)** $\mathrm{R}:(\mathrm{R}-\mathrm{d})$
- **(C)** $\mathrm{R}:(\mathrm{R}-\mathrm{d})^2$
- **(D)** $\mathrm{R}:(\mathrm{R}+\mathrm{d})^2$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) $\mathrm{R}:(\mathrm{R}-\mathrm{d})$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The height of capillary rise is given by $h = \dfrac{2T \cos \theta}{r \rho g}$

This implies $h \propto \dfrac{1}{g}$

At the surface of the earth, the capillary rise is $x \propto \dfrac{1}{g}$

At a depth $d$ in a mine, the acceleration due to gravity is $g' = g\left(1 - \dfrac{d}{R}\right) = g\left(\dfrac{R-d}{R}\right)$

The capillary rise at depth $d$ is $Y \propto \dfrac{1}{g'}$

Taking the ratio of $Y$ to $x$:

$\dfrac{Y}{x} = \dfrac{g}{g'} = \dfrac{g}{g\left(\dfrac{R-d}{R}\right)} = \dfrac{R}{R-d}$

Thus, $Y:x = R:(R-d)$

Answer: $\mathrm{R}:(\mathrm{R}-\mathrm{d})$

</details>

---

### Q77. MHT CET 2025 (21 Apr Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

A liquid drop of volume $V$ is placed on the surface of glass plate. Then another glass plate is placed on it such that liquid forms a thin layer of area A between the surfaces of two plates. To separate the plates a force $F$ has to be applied normal to the surfaces. The surface tension of the liquid is

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\frac{\mathrm{FV}}{2 \mathrm{~A}}$
- **(B)** $\frac{F V}{2 A^2}$
- **(C)** $\frac{F V}{A^2}$
- **(D)** $\frac{F}{V A}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) $\frac{F V}{2 A^2}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Let $t$ be the thickness of the liquid layer between the two glass plates.

The volume of the liquid is given by $V = A t \Rightarrow t = \dfrac{V}{A}$.

The radius of curvature of the meniscus formed at the edge of the liquid layer is $r = \dfrac{t}{2}$.

The pressure difference across the free surface of the liquid is $\Delta P = \dfrac{T}{r} = \dfrac{2T}{t}$, where $T$ is the surface tension.

The force required to separate the two glass plates is $F = \Delta P \times A$.

Substituting the value of $\Delta P$, we get $F = \dfrac{2T A}{t}$.

Substituting $t = \dfrac{V}{A}$, we get $F = \dfrac{2T A^2}{V}$.

Rearranging for surface tension $T$, we get $T = \dfrac{F V}{2 A^2}$.

Answer: $\frac{F V}{2 A^2}$

</details>

---

### Q78. MHT CET 2025 (20 Apr Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

Pure water rises through a height $h$ in a capillary tube of internal radius $r$. Surface tension of water is $T$. The pressure difference between the water level in the container and the lowest point of the concave meniscus is

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\frac{\mathrm{r}}{\mathrm{T}}$
- **(B)** $\frac{\mathrm{T}}{\mathrm{r}}$
- **(C)** $\frac{2 \mathrm{~T}}{\mathrm{r}}$
- **(D)** $\frac{\mathrm{r}}{2 \mathrm{~T}}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) $\frac{2 \mathrm{~T}}{\mathrm{r}}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The pressure at the free surface of water in the container is atmospheric pressure, $P_0$.

For a concave meniscus in a capillary tube, the pressure just below the lowest point of the meniscus is less than the pressure just above it (which is $P_0$) by an amount equal to the excess pressure.

According to Laplace's law, the excess pressure across a curved liquid surface is given by $\Delta P = \dfrac{2T}{R}$, where $R$ is the radius of curvature of the meniscus.

For pure water, the angle of contact is $\theta = 0^{\circ}$. Thus, the radius of curvature of the meniscus is equal to the internal radius of the capillary tube, $R = r$.

The pressure just below the meniscus is $P_0 - \dfrac{2T}{r}$.

Therefore, the pressure difference between the water level in the container and the lowest point of the concave meniscus is:

$\Delta P = P_0 - \left(P_0 - \dfrac{2T}{r}\right) = \dfrac{2T}{r}$

Answer: $\frac{2 \mathrm{~T}}{\mathrm{r}}$

</details>

---

### Q79. MHT CET 2025 (20 Apr Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

A liquid drop having surface energy E is sprayed into 512 droplets of same size. The final surface energy is

**Options:**

- **(A)** 12 E
- **(B)** 4 E
- **(C)** 8 E
- **(D)** 6 E

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) 8 E**

**Detailed Solution:**

Let the initial radius of the liquid drop be $R$ and the surface tension be $T$.

Initial surface energy $E = 4\pi R^2 T$.

Let the radius of each of the $512$ smaller droplets be $r$.

Since the total volume remains constant during the spraying process:

$\dfrac{4}{3}\pi R^3 = 512 \times \dfrac{4}{3}\pi r^3$

$R^3 = 512 r^3$

$R = 8r \Rightarrow r = \dfrac{R}{8}$

The final surface energy $E'$ of the $512$ droplets is:

$E' = 512 \times 4\pi r^2 T$

Substituting $r = \dfrac{R}{8}$:

$E' = 512 \times 4\pi \left(\dfrac{R}{8}\right)^2 T$

$E' = 512 \times \dfrac{4\pi R^2 T}{64}$

$E' = 8 \times (4\pi R^2 T)$

$E' = 8E$

Answer: 8 E

</details>

---

### Q80. MHT CET 2025 (20 Apr Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

A water drop of $0.01 \mathrm{~cm}^3$ is squeezed between two glass plates and spreads in to area of $10 \mathrm{~cm}^2$. If surface tension of water is 70 dyne $/ \mathrm{cm}$ then the normal force required to separate glass plates from each other will be

**Options:**

- **(A)** 12 N
- **(B)** 14 N
- **(C)** 16 N
- **(D)** 28 N

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) 14 N**

**Detailed Solution:**

Volume of the water drop, $V = 0.01 \mathrm{~cm}^3$
Area of the film, $A = 10 \mathrm{~cm}^2$
Surface tension, $T = 70 \text{ dyne/cm}$

Let the thickness of the water film be $t$. The volume of the film is given by:
$V = A \times t \Rightarrow t = \dfrac{V}{A}$

The pressure difference across the free surface of the water film is $\Delta P = \dfrac{2T}{t}$.
The normal force required to separate the glass plates is:
$F = \Delta P \times A = \dfrac{2AT}{t}$

Substituting $t = \dfrac{V}{A}$ into the force equation:
$F = \dfrac{2A^2T}{V}$

Substituting the given values:
$F = \dfrac{2 \times (10)^2 \times 70}{0.01}$
$F = \dfrac{14000}{0.01} = 14 \times 10^5 \text{ dyne}$

Since $1 \text{ N} = 10^5 \text{ dyne}$, we get:
$F = 14 \text{ N}$

Answer: 14 N

</details>

---

### Q81. MHT CET 2025 (20 Apr Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

A liquid drop having surface energy E is spread into 729 droplets of same size. The final surface energy of the droplets is

**Options:**

- **(A)** 6 E
- **(B)** 9 E
- **(C)** E
- **(D)** 3 E

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) 9 E**

**Detailed Solution:**

Let the radius of the initial drop be $R$ and the radius of each small droplet be $r$.

Initial surface energy is $E = 4\pi R^2 T$.

By conservation of volume:
$\dfrac{4}{3}\pi R^3 = 729 \times \dfrac{4}{3}\pi r^3$

$R^3 = 729 r^3 \Rightarrow R = 9r \Rightarrow r = \dfrac{R}{9}$

Final surface energy of 729 droplets is:
$E_f = 729 \times 4\pi r^2 T$

$E_f = 729 \times 4\pi \left(\dfrac{R}{9}\right)^2 T$

$E_f = 729 \times \dfrac{4\pi R^2 T}{81}$

$E_f = 9 \times (4\pi R^2 T) = 9E$

Answer: 9 E

</details>

---

### Q82. MHT CET 2025 (20 Apr Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

The surface energy of a liquid drop is "V". It is sprayed into 1000 equal droplets. The surface energy of all the droplets is

**Options:**

- **(A)** V
- **(B)** 10 V
- **(C)** 100 V
- **(D)** 1000 V

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) 10 V**

**Detailed Solution:**

Let the radius of the initial liquid drop be $R$ and the surface tension be $T$.

The initial surface energy is given by $V = 4\pi R^2 T$.

When the drop is sprayed into $n = 1000$ equal droplets of radius $r$, the total volume remains constant.

$\dfrac{4}{3}\pi R^3 = 1000 \times \dfrac{4}{3}\pi r^3$

$R^3 = 1000 r^3 \Rightarrow r = \dfrac{R}{10}$

The total surface energy of the $1000$ droplets is $E = 1000 \times 4\pi r^2 T$.

Substituting $r = \dfrac{R}{10}$:

$E = 1000 \times 4\pi \left(\dfrac{R}{10}\right)^2 T$

$E = 1000 \times \dfrac{4\pi R^2 T}{100}$

$E = 10 \times (4\pi R^2 T) = 10 V$

Answer: 10 V

</details>

### Q83. MHT CET 2025 (19 Apr Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

When a big drop of water is formed from ' $n$ ' small drops of water, the energy loss is '3E' where ' E ' is the energy of the bigger drop. The radius of the bigger drop is ' $R$ ' and that of smaller drop is ' $r$ ' then the value of ' n ' is

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\frac{2 \mathrm{R}^2}{\mathrm{r}}$
- **(B)** $\frac{4 R^2}{r^2}$
- **(C)** $\frac{4 \mathrm{R}}{\mathrm{r}}$
- **(D)** $\frac{4 \mathrm{R}}{\mathrm{r}^2}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) $\frac{4 R^2}{r^2}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Let $T$ be the surface tension of water.

Surface energy of the bigger drop, $E = 4 \pi R^2 T$

Surface energy of $n$ smaller drops, $E_i = n(4 \pi r^2 T)$

Energy loss during the formation of the bigger drop is given by:

$\Delta E = E_i - E$

Given that the energy loss is $3E$:

$E_i - E = 3E$

$E_i = 4E$

Substituting the expressions for $E_i$ and $E$:

$n(4 \pi r^2 T) = 4(4 \pi R^2 T)$

$n r^2 = 4 R^2$

$n = \dfrac{4 R^2}{r^2}$

Answer: $\frac{4 R^2}{r^2}$

</details>

---

### Q84. MHT CET 2025 (19 Apr Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `3`

One end of a capillary tube is dipped in water, the rise of water column is ' $h$ '. The upward force of 98 dyne due to surface tension is balanced by the force due to the weight of the water column. The inner circumference of the capillary is (surface tension of water $=7 \times 10^{-2} \mathrm{Nm}^{-1}$ )

**Options:**

- **(A)** 1.4 cm
- **(B)** 0.7 cm
- **(C)** 0.14 cm
- **(D)** 0.07 cm

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) 1.4 cm**

**Detailed Solution:**

The upward force due to surface tension is given by $F = T \times C \times \cos \theta$, where $C$ is the inner circumference of the capillary tube and $\theta$ is the angle of contact.

For water, the angle of contact is $\theta = 0^\circ$, so $\cos \theta = 1$.

Given $F = 98 \text{ dyne} = 98 \times 10^{-5} \text{ N}$ and $T = 7 \times 10^{-2} \text{ N m}^{-1}$.

Substituting the values, we get:

$98 \times 10^{-5} = 7 \times 10^{-2} \times C$

$C = \dfrac{98 \times 10^{-5}}{7 \times 10^{-2}} \text{ m}$

$C = 14 \times 10^{-3} \text{ m}$

Converting to centimeters:

$C = 14 \times 10^{-3} \times 10^2 \text{ cm} = 1.4 \text{ cm}$

Answer: 1.4 cm

</details>

---

### Q85. MHT CET 2025 (19 Apr Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

A liquid rises to a height of 2.4 cm in a glass capillary $P$. Another glass capillary Q having diameter 80% of capillary P is immersed in the same liquid. The rise of liquid in capillary Q is

**Options:**

- **(A)** $2.4 \mathrm{~cm}$
- **(B)** $3.4 \mathrm{~cm}$
- **(C)** 3 cm
- **(D)** $2.5 \mathrm{~cm}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) 3 cm**

**Detailed Solution:**

The height of liquid rise in a capillary tube is given by

$h = \dfrac{2T \cos \theta}{r \rho g}$

For the same liquid and glass interface, $h \propto \dfrac{1}{r}$ or $h \propto \dfrac{1}{D}$, where $D$ is the diameter of the capillary tube.

Therefore, $h_P D_P = h_Q D_Q$

Given that $D_Q = 0.8 D_P$ and $h_P = 2.4$ cm.

$h_Q = h_P \left( \dfrac{D_P}{D_Q} \right)$

$h_Q = 2.4 \left( \dfrac{D_P}{0.8 D_P} \right)$

$h_Q = \dfrac{2.4}{0.8} = 3$ cm

Answer: 3 cm

</details>

---

### Q86. MHT CET 2024 (16 May Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

A liquid drop having surface energy ' $E$ ' is spread into 512 droplets of same size. The final surface energy of the droplets is

**Options:**

- **(A)** 2 E
- **(B)** 4 E
- **(C)** 8 E
- **(D)** 12 E

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) 8 E**

**Detailed Solution:**

Let the radius of the initial drop be $R$ and the radius of each small droplet be $r$.

Initial surface energy of the drop is given by:
$E = T \times 4\pi R^2$

By conservation of volume:
$\dfrac{4}{3}\pi R^3 = 512 \times \dfrac{4}{3}\pi r^3$

$R^3 = 512 r^3 \Rightarrow R = 8r \Rightarrow r = \dfrac{R}{8}$

Final surface energy of the 512 droplets is:
$E' = 512 \times T \times 4\pi r^2$

Substituting $r = \dfrac{R}{8}$:
$E' = 512 \times T \times 4\pi \left(\dfrac{R}{8}\right)^2$

$E' = 512 \times T \times 4\pi \dfrac{R^2}{64}$

$E' = 8 \times (T \times 4\pi R^2) = 8E$

Answer: 8 E

</details>

### Q87. MHT CET 2024 (16 May Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

A water drop is divided into 8 equal droplets. The pressure difference between the inner and outer side of the big drop will be

**Options:**

- **(A)** same as the smaller droplet.
- **(B)** half of that for smaller droplet
- **(C)** $\left(\frac{1}{4}\right)^{\text {th }}$ of that for smaller droplet.
- **(D)** twice that for smaller droplet

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) half of that for smaller droplet**

**Detailed Solution:**

Let the radius of the big drop be $R$ and the radius of each small droplet be $r$.

Since the volume remains constant when the drop is divided:

$\dfrac{4}{3}\pi R^3 = 8 \times \dfrac{4}{3}\pi r^3$

$R^3 = 8r^3 \Rightarrow R = 2r$

The pressure difference (excess pressure) for a liquid drop is given by $\Delta P = \dfrac{2T}{R}$, where $T$ is the surface tension.

For the big drop, the pressure difference is:

$\Delta P_{\text{big}} = \dfrac{2T}{R}$

For the smaller droplet, the pressure difference is:

$\Delta P_{\text{small}} = \dfrac{2T}{r}$

Substituting $R = 2r$ in the expression for the big drop:

$\Delta P_{\text{big}} = \dfrac{2T}{2r} = \dfrac{1}{2} \left(\dfrac{2T}{r}\right) = \dfrac{1}{2} \Delta P_{\text{small}}$

Thus, the pressure difference for the big drop is half of that for the smaller droplet.

Answer: half of that for smaller droplet

</details>

---

### Q88. MHT CET 2024 (16 May Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

On the surface of the liquid in equilibrium, molecules of the liquid possess

**Options:**

- **(A)** maximum potential energy.
- **(B)** minimum potential energy.
- **(C)** maximum kinetic energy.
- **(D)** minimum kinetic energy.

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) maximum potential energy.**

**Detailed Solution:**

Molecules on the surface of a liquid experience a net inward cohesive force.

To bring a molecule from the interior of the liquid to the surface, work must be done against this net inward force.

This work done is stored in the molecule in the form of potential energy.

Therefore, the molecules on the surface of the liquid possess maximum potential energy as compared to the molecules in the interior.

Answer: maximum potential energy.

</details>

---

### Q89. MHT CET 2024 (16 May Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

When capillary is dipped vertically in water, rise of water in capillary is ' h '. The angle of contact is zero. Now the tube is depressed so that its length above the water surface is $\frac{\mathrm{h}}{3}$. The new apparent angle of contact is $\left(\cos 0^{\circ}=1\right)$

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\cos ^{-1}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)$
- **(B)** $\quad \cos ^{-1}\left(\frac{1}{3}\right)$
- **(C)** $\quad \cos ^{-1}\left(\frac{1}{4}\right)$
- **(D)** $\quad \cos ^{-1}\left(\frac{1}{6}\right)$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) $\quad \cos ^{-1}\left(\frac{1}{3}\right)$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The height of capillary rise is given by $h = \dfrac{2T \cos \theta}{r \rho g}$.

For a given liquid and capillary tube, $\dfrac{h}{\cos \theta} = \text{constant}$.

Initially, the angle of contact is $\theta = 0^{\circ}$, so $\cos \theta = 1$.

When the tube is depressed, the liquid does not overflow but the meniscus adjusts its shape. The new height of the liquid column is $h' = \dfrac{h}{3}$.

Let the new apparent angle of contact be $\theta'$.

Using the relation $\dfrac{h}{\cos \theta} = \dfrac{h'}{\cos \theta'}$, we get:

$\dfrac{h}{1} = \dfrac{\dfrac{h}{3}}{\cos \theta'}$

$\cos \theta' = \dfrac{1}{3}$

$\theta' = \cos^{-1}\left(\dfrac{1}{3}\right)$

Answer: $\quad \cos ^{-1}\left(\frac{1}{3}\right)$

</details>

---

### Q90. MHT CET 2024 (16 May Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

In most liquids, with rise in temperature, surface tension of a liquid

**Options:**

- **(A)** first decreases and then increases.
- **(B)** increases.
- **(C)** decreases.
- **(D)** remains unchanged.

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) decreases.**

**Detailed Solution:**

With a rise in temperature, the kinetic energy of the liquid molecules increases. This increased thermal agitation weakens the intermolecular forces of attraction (cohesive forces) between the molecules.

Since surface tension is a direct consequence of these cohesive forces, a decrease in the cohesive forces leads to a decrease in surface tension.

Therefore, in most liquids, the surface tension decreases with an increase in temperature and becomes zero at the critical temperature.

Answer: decreases.

</details>

---

### Q91. MHT CET 2024 (15 May Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

Two capillary tubes A and B of the same internal diameter are kept vertically in two different liquids whose densities are in the ratio $4: 3$. If the surface tensions of these two liquids are in the ratio $6: 5$, then the ratio of rise of liquid in capillary A to that in B is (assume their angles of contact are nearly equal)

**Options:**

- **(A)** 10:9
- **(B)** $9: 10$
- **(C)** 7:10
- **(D)** $10: 7$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) $9: 10$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The formula for capillary rise is given by $h = \dfrac{2T \cos \theta}{r \rho g}$

Since the internal diameters are the same, $r_A = r_B$. The angles of contact are nearly equal, so $\cos \theta_A = \cos \theta_B$.

The ratio of capillary rise is:

$\dfrac{h_A}{h_B} = \dfrac{T_A}{T_B} \times \dfrac{\rho_B}{\rho_A}$

Given $\dfrac{T_A}{T_B} = \dfrac{6}{5}$ and $\dfrac{\rho_A}{\rho_B} = \dfrac{4}{3}$

$\dfrac{h_A}{h_B} = \dfrac{6}{5} \times \dfrac{3}{4} = \dfrac{18}{20} = \dfrac{9}{10}$

Answer: $9: 10$

</details>

---

### Q92. MHT CET 2024 (15 May Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

The angle of contact between glass and water is $0^{\circ}$ and water rises in a glass capillary upto 6 cm (Surface tension of water is T). Another liquid of surface tension ' $2 \mathrm{~T}^{\prime}$, angle of contact $60^{\circ}$ and relative density 2 will rise in the same capillary up to $\left(\cos 0^{\circ}=1, \cos 60^{\circ}=0.5\right)$

**Options:**

- **(A)** 1.5 cm
- **(B)** 2 cm
- **(C)** 3 cm
- **(D)** 4.0 cm

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) 3 cm**

**Detailed Solution:**

The height of liquid in a capillary tube is given by Jurin's law:

$h = \dfrac{2T \cos \theta}{r \rho g}$

For water:
$h_1 = 6 \text{ cm}$
$T_1 = T$
$\theta_1 = 0^{\circ}$
$\rho_1 = \rho_w$

For the second liquid:
$T_2 = 2T$
$\theta_2 = 60^{\circ}$
$\rho_2 = 2\rho_w$

Taking the ratio of the heights for the same capillary tube ($r_1 = r_2 = r$):

$\dfrac{h_2}{h_1} = \dfrac{T_2}{T_1} \times \dfrac{\cos \theta_2}{\cos \theta_1} \times \dfrac{\rho_1}{\rho_2}$

Substituting the given values:

$\dfrac{h_2}{6} = \left(\dfrac{2T}{T}\right) \times \left(\dfrac{\cos 60^{\circ}}{\cos 0^{\circ}}\right) \times \left(\dfrac{\rho_w}{2\rho_w}\right)$

$\dfrac{h_2}{6} = 2 \times \dfrac{0.5}{1} \times \dfrac{1}{2}$

$\dfrac{h_2}{6} = 0.5$

$h_2 = 3 \text{ cm}$

Answer: 3 cm

</details>

---

### Q93. MHT CET 2024 (15 May Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

When a mercury drop of radius ' $R$ ' splits up into 1000 droplets of radius ' $r$ ', the change in surface energy is ( $T=$ surface tension of mercury)

**Options:**

- **(A)** $8 \pi \mathrm{R}^2 \mathrm{~T}$
- **(B)** $16 \pi \mathrm{R}^2 \mathrm{~T}$
- **(C)** $\quad 34 \pi \mathrm{R}^2 \mathrm{~T}$
- **(D)** $\quad 36 \pi R^2 T$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(D) $\quad 36 \pi R^2 T$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Volume of the large drop is equal to the total volume of 1000 small droplets.

$\dfrac{4}{3} \pi R^3 = 1000 \times \dfrac{4}{3} \pi r^3$

$R^3 = 1000 r^3$

$R = 10 r \Rightarrow r = \dfrac{R}{10}$

Initial surface area, $A_1 = 4 \pi R^2$

Final surface area, $A_2 = 1000 \times 4 \pi r^2$

$A_2 = 1000 \times 4 \pi \left(\dfrac{R}{10}\right)^2 = 40 \pi R^2$

Change in surface area, $\Delta A = A_2 - A_1$

$\Delta A = 40 \pi R^2 - 4 \pi R^2 = 36 \pi R^2$

Change in surface energy, $\Delta E = T \Delta A = 36 \pi R^2 T$

Answer: $\quad 36 \pi R^2 T$

</details>

---

### Q94. MHT CET 2024 (11 May Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

A capillary tube stands with its lower end dipped into liquid for which the angle of contact is $90^{\circ}$. The liquid will

**Options:**

- **(A)** neither rise nor fall.
- **(B)** get depressed only.
- **(C)** rise only.
- **(D)** rise upto the top of the tube.

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) neither rise nor fall.**

**Detailed Solution:**

The height of the liquid column in a capillary tube is given by Jurin's law:

$h = \dfrac{2T \cos \theta}{r \rho g}$

where $T$ is the surface tension, $\theta$ is the angle of contact, $r$ is the radius of the capillary tube, $\rho$ is the density of the liquid, and $g$ is the acceleration due to gravity.

Given that the angle of contact $\theta = 90^{\circ}$.

Substituting $\theta = 90^{\circ}$ in the formula, we get:

$h = \dfrac{2T \cos 90^{\circ}}{r \rho g}$

Since $\cos 90^{\circ} = 0$, we have:

$h = 0$

Thus, the liquid will neither rise nor fall in the capillary tube.

Answer: neither rise nor fall.

</details>

---

### Q95. MHT CET 2024 (11 May Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

The work done in blowing a soap bubble of radius R is $\mathrm{W}_1$ at room temperature. Now the soap solution is heated. From the heated solution another soap bubble of radius 2 R is blown and the work done is $\mathrm{W}_2$. Then

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\mathrm{W}_2=0$
- **(B)** $\mathrm{W}_2=4 \mathrm{~W}_1$
- **(C)** $\mathrm{W}_2 \lt 4 \mathrm{~W}_1$
- **(D)** $\quad \mathrm{W}_2=\mathrm{W}_1$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) $\mathrm{W}_2 \lt 4 \mathrm{~W}_1$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The work done in blowing a soap bubble of radius $R$ is given by $W = 2 \times 4\pi R^2 \times T = 8\pi R^2 T$, where $T$ is the surface tension of the soap solution.

At room temperature, the work done is $W_1 = 8\pi R^2 T_1$.

When the soap solution is heated, its surface tension decreases. Let the new surface tension at the higher temperature be $T_2$. Thus, $T_2 < T_1$.

The work done in blowing a new bubble of radius $2R$ from the heated solution is:

$W_2 = 8\pi (2R)^2 T_2 = 32\pi R^2 T_2 = 4(8\pi R^2 T_2)$

Since $T_2 < T_1$, we can write:

$4(8\pi R^2 T_2) < 4(8\pi R^2 T_1)$

Therefore, $W_2 < 4W_1$.

Answer: $\mathrm{W}_2 \lt 4 \mathrm{~W}_1$

</details>

---

### Q96. MHT CET 2024 (11 May Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

Water rises in a capillary tube of radius ' $r$ ' up to height ' $h$ '. The mass of water in capillary is ' $m$ '. The mass of water that will rise in capillary of radius $\mathrm{r} / 3$ will be

**Options:**

- **(A)** m
- **(B)** $\frac{\mathrm{m}}{3}$
- **(C)** $\frac{m}{6}$
- **(D)** $\frac{\mathrm{m}}{9}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) $\frac{\mathrm{m}}{3}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The height of water in a capillary tube is given by $h = \dfrac{2T \cos \theta}{r \rho g}$.

The mass of water in the capillary tube is $m = \pi r^2 h \rho$.

Substituting the value of $h$, we get:

$m = \pi r^2 \left( \dfrac{2T \cos \theta}{r \rho g} \right) \rho = \dfrac{2 \pi r T \cos \theta}{g}$

This implies that $m \propto r$.

For a capillary tube of radius $r' = \dfrac{r}{3}$, the new mass $m'$ is:

$m' = \dfrac{m}{3}$

Answer: $\frac{\mathrm{m}}{3}$

</details>

---

### Q97. MHT CET 2024 (11 May Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

A big water drop is formed by the combination of ' $n$ ' small water droplets of equal radii. The ratio of the surface energy of ' $n$ ' droplets to the surface energy of the big drop is

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\sqrt{\mathrm{n}}: 1$
- **(B)** $\sqrt[3]{\mathrm{n}}: 1$
- **(C)** $n: 1$
- **(D)** $\mathrm{n}^2: 1$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) $\sqrt[3]{\mathrm{n}}: 1$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Let $r$ be the radius of each small droplet and $R$ be the radius of the big drop.

By conservation of volume:
$n \times \dfrac{4}{3} \pi r^3 = \dfrac{4}{3} \pi R^3$
$R = n^{1/3} r$

Surface energy of $n$ small droplets is $E_1 = n \times 4 \pi r^2 T$
Surface energy of the big drop is $E_2 = 4 \pi R^2 T$

The required ratio is:
$\dfrac{E_1}{E_2} = \dfrac{n \times 4 \pi r^2 T}{4 \pi R^2 T} = \dfrac{n r^2}{R^2}$

Substituting $R = n^{1/3} r$:
$\dfrac{E_1}{E_2} = \dfrac{n r^2}{(n^{1/3} r)^2} = \dfrac{n}{n^{2/3}} = n^{1/3}$

The ratio is $\sqrt[3]{n} : 1$.

Answer: $\sqrt[3]{\mathrm{n}}: 1$

</details>

---

### Q98. MHT CET 2024 (10 May Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

A glass capillary of radius $0.35\text{ mm}$ is inclined at $60^\circ$ with the vertical in water. The length of the water column in the capillary is (surface tension of water = $7 \times 10^{-2}\text{ N/m}$, acceleration due to gravity, g = $10\text{ m/s}^2$, $\cos 0^\circ = 1$, $\cos 60^\circ = 0.5$)

**Options:**

- **(A)** 6 cm
- **(B)** 8 cm
- **(C)** 10 cm
- **(D)** 12 cm

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) 8 cm**

**Detailed Solution:**

The vertical height $h$ to which water rises in a capillary tube is given by

$h = \dfrac{2T \cos \theta}{r \rho g}$

Substituting the given values:

$h = \dfrac{2 \times 7 \times 10^{-2} \times 1}{0.35 \times 10^{-3} \times 1000 \times 10}$

$h = \dfrac{14 \times 10^{-2}}{3.5} = 0.04 \text{ m} = 4 \text{ cm}$

When the capillary tube is inclined at an angle $\alpha$ with the vertical, the vertical height of the liquid column remains the same. If $l$ is the length of the water column in the inclined tube, then

$h = l \cos \alpha$

$l = \dfrac{h}{\cos 60^\circ}$

$l = \dfrac{4}{0.5} = 8 \text{ cm}$

Answer: 8 cm

</details>

---

### Q99. MHT CET 2024 (10 May Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

Two soap bubbles having radii ' $r_1$ ' and ' $r_2$ ' has inside pressure ' $\mathrm{P}_1$ ' and ' $\mathrm{P}_2$ ' respectively. If $\mathrm{P}_0$ is external pressure then ratio of their volume is

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\dfrac{\left(\mathrm{P}_1-\mathrm{P}_0\right)}{\left(\mathrm{P}_2-\mathrm{P}_0\right)}$
- **(B)** $\dfrac{\left(\mathrm{P}_2-\mathrm{P}_0\right)}{\left(\mathrm{P}_1-\mathrm{P}_0\right)}$
- **(C)** $\dfrac{\left(\mathrm{P}_2-\mathrm{P}_0\right)^3}{\left(\mathrm{P}_1-\mathrm{P}_0\right)^3}$
- **(D)** $\dfrac{\left(\mathrm{P}_1-\mathrm{P}_0\right)^3}{\left(\mathrm{P}_2-\mathrm{P}_0\right)^3}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) $\dfrac{\left(\mathrm{P}_2-\mathrm{P}_0\right)^3}{\left(\mathrm{P}_1-\mathrm{P}_0\right)^3}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The excess pressure inside a soap bubble is given by $\Delta P = \dfrac{4T}{r}$.

For the first bubble, $P_1 - P_0 = \dfrac{4T}{r_1} \Rightarrow r_1 = \dfrac{4T}{P_1 - P_0}$.

For the second bubble, $P_2 - P_0 = \dfrac{4T}{r_2} \Rightarrow r_2 = \dfrac{4T}{P_2 - P_0}$.

The volume of a spherical bubble is $V = \dfrac{4}{3}\pi r^3$.

The ratio of their volumes is $\dfrac{V_1}{V_2} = \left(\dfrac{r_1}{r_2}\right)^3$.

Substituting the values of $r_1$ and $r_2$, we get $\dfrac{V_1}{V_2} = \left(\dfrac{\dfrac{4T}{P_1 - P_0}}{\dfrac{4T}{P_2 - P_0}}\right)^3 = \dfrac{(P_2 - P_0)^3}{(P_1 - P_0)^3}$.

Answer: $\dfrac{\left(\mathrm{P}_2-\mathrm{P}_0\right)^3}{\left(\mathrm{P}_1-\mathrm{P}_0\right)^3}$

</details>

---

### Q100. MHT CET 2024 (09 May Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

The work done in splitting a water drop of radius R into 64 droplets is ( $\mathrm{T}=$ Surface tension of water)

**Options:**

- **(A)** $6 \pi \mathrm{TR}^2$
- **(B)** $12 \pi \mathrm{TR}^2$
- **(C)** $8 \pi \mathrm{TR}^2$
- **(D)** $\quad 24 \pi \mathrm{TR}^2$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) $12 \pi \mathrm{TR}^2$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Let the radius of the large drop be $R$ and the radius of each small droplet be $r$.

Since the volume remains constant during the splitting process:

$\dfrac{4}{3} \pi R^3 = 64 \times \dfrac{4}{3} \pi r^3$

$R^3 = 64 r^3 \Rightarrow r = \dfrac{R}{4}$

Initial surface area of the large drop:

$A_i = 4 \pi R^2$

Final surface area of the 64 small droplets:

$A_f = 64 \times 4 \pi r^2 = 64 \times 4 \pi \left(\dfrac{R}{4}\right)^2 = 16 \pi R^2$

Change in surface area:

$\Delta A = A_f - A_i = 16 \pi R^2 - 4 \pi R^2 = 12 \pi R^2$

Work done in splitting the drop is equal to the product of surface tension and the change in surface area:

$W = \mathrm{T} \Delta A = 12 \pi \mathrm{T} R^2$

Answer: $12 \pi \mathrm{TR}^2$

</details>

---

### Q101. MHT CET 2024 (09 May Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

Let ' $n$ ' is the number of liquid drops, each with surface energy ' $E$ '. These drops join to form single drop. In this process

**Options:**

- **(A)** some energy will be absorbed
- **(B)** energy absorbed is $\left[E\left(n-n^{2 / 3}\right)\right]$
- **(C)** energy released will be $\left[E\left(n-n^{2 / 3}\right)\right]$
- **(D)** energy released will be $\left[\mathrm{E}\left(2^{2 / 3}-1\right)\right]$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) energy released will be $\left[E\left(n-n^{2 / 3}\right)\right]$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Let $r$ be the radius of each small drop and $R$ be the radius of the large drop.

Since volume remains constant during the process:

$\dfrac{4}{3}\pi R^3 = n \times \dfrac{4}{3}\pi r^3$

$R = n^{1/3}r$

The surface energy of one small drop is $E = 4\pi r^2 T$, where $T$ is the surface tension.

Initial total surface energy of $n$ drops:

$E_i = n \times (4\pi r^2 T) = nE$

Final surface energy of the single large drop:

$E_f = 4\pi R^2 T = 4\pi (n^{1/3}r)^2 T = n^{2/3}(4\pi r^2 T) = n^{2/3}E$

Since $E_i > E_f$, the surface energy decreases, which means energy is released.

Energy released $= E_i - E_f = nE - n^{2/3}E = E(n - n^{2/3})$

Answer: energy released will be $\left[E\left(n-n^{2 / 3}\right)\right]$

</details>

---

### Q102. MHT CET 2024 (09 May Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `3`

Three liquids of densities $\rho_1, \rho_2$ and $\rho_3$ (with $\rho_1\gt\rho_2\gt\rho_3$ ) having same value of surface tension T , rise to the same height in three identical capillaries. Angle of contact $\theta_1, \theta_2$ and $\theta_3$ respectively obey

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\dfrac{\pi}{2}\gt\theta_1\gt\theta_2\gt\theta_3\gt0$
- **(B)** $0 \leqslant \theta_1 \lt \theta_2 \lt \theta_3 \lt \dfrac{\pi}{2}$
- **(C)** $\dfrac{\pi}{2} \lt \theta_1 \lt \theta_2 \lt \theta_3 \lt \pi$
- **(D)** $\pi\gt\theta_1\gt\theta_2\gt\dfrac{\pi}{2}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) $0 \leqslant \theta_1 \lt \theta_2 \lt \theta_3 \lt \dfrac{\pi}{2}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The height of capillary rise is given by Jurin's law:

$h = \dfrac{2T \cos\theta}{\rho g r}$

Rearranging the formula for $\cos\theta$, we get:

$\cos\theta = \dfrac{h r g}{2T} \rho$

Since the liquids rise to the same height $h$ in identical capillaries (same radius $r$) and have the same surface tension $T$, the term $\dfrac{h r g}{2T}$ is a positive constant. Thus, $\cos\theta \propto \rho$.

Given the relation between densities is $\rho_1 \gt \rho_2 \gt \rho_3$, it follows that:

$\cos\theta_1 \gt \cos\theta_2 \gt \cos\theta_3$

Since the liquids rise in the capillary ($h \gt 0$), $\cos\theta$ must be positive, meaning the angles of contact are acute, i.e., $0 \leqslant \theta \lt \dfrac{\pi}{2}$.

In the interval $[0, \dfrac{\pi}{2})$, the cosine function is strictly decreasing. Therefore, a larger value of cosine corresponds to a smaller angle. This gives:

$\theta_1 \lt \theta_2 \lt \theta_3$

Combining the conditions, we get:

$0 \leqslant \theta_1 \lt \theta_2 \lt \theta_3 \lt \dfrac{\pi}{2}$

Answer: $0 \leqslant \theta_1 \lt \theta_2 \lt \theta_3 \lt \dfrac{\pi}{2}$

</details>

---

### Q103. MHT CET 2024 (09 May Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

When an air bubble rises from the bottom of lake to the surface, its radius is doubled. The atmospheric pressure is equal to that of a column of water of height ' $H$ '. The depth of the lake is

**Options:**

- **(A)** H
- **(B)** 2 H
- **(C)** 7 H
- **(D)** 8 H

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) 7 H**

**Detailed Solution:**

Let the radius of the bubble at the bottom be $r$ and at the surface be $2r$.

Volume of the bubble at the bottom, $V_1 = \dfrac{4}{3}\pi r^3$

Volume of the bubble at the surface, $V_2 = \dfrac{4}{3}\pi (2r)^3 = 8V_1$

Let the depth of the lake be $h$. The atmospheric pressure is given as $P_2 = \rho g H$, where $\rho$ is the density of water.

Pressure at the bottom of the lake, $P_1 = P_{atm} + \rho g h = \rho g H + \rho g h = \rho g(H + h)$

Assuming the temperature of the lake is constant, we apply Boyle's law:

$P_1 V_1 = P_2 V_2$

$\rho g(H + h) \times V_1 = \rho g H \times 8V_1$

$H + h = 8H$

$h = 7H$

Answer: 7 H

</details>

---

### Q104. MHT CET 2024 (04 May Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

Identify the correct figure which shows the relation between the height of water column in a capillary tube and the capillary radius.
<img src="https://cdn-question-pool.getmarks.app/pyq/mht_cet/vnu-B5xGkQYnkHpm9jmmZ6f975Cn-gxouhUga6H-r40.original.fullsize.png"/>

**Options:**

- **(A)** (ii)
- **(B)** (iv)
- **(C)** (i)
- **(D)** (iii)

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) (ii)**

**Detailed Solution:**

The height $h$ of the liquid column in a capillary tube is given by Jurin's law:

$h = \dfrac{2T \cos \theta}{\rho g r}$

where $T$ is the surface tension, $\theta$ is the angle of contact, $\rho$ is the density of the liquid, $g$ is the acceleration due to gravity, and $r$ is the radius of the capillary tube.

For a given liquid and material of the capillary tube, $T$, $\theta$, $\rho$, and $g$ are constants. Therefore, the height $h$ is inversely proportional to the radius $r$:

$h \propto \dfrac{1}{r}$

This inverse relationship means that the graph between $h$ and $r$ is a rectangular hyperbola. Looking at the given figures, graph (ii) correctly represents this rectangular hyperbolic relation.

Answer: (ii)

</details>

### Q105. MHT CET 2024 (04 May Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

A water film is formed between two parallel wires of 10 cm length. The distance of 0.5 cm between the wires is increased by 1 mm . The work done in the process is (surface tension of water $=72 \mathrm{~N} / \mathrm{m}$ ).

**Options:**

- **(A)** $2.88 \times 10^{-2} \mathrm{~J}$
- **(B)** $7.2 \times 10^{-2} \mathrm{~J}$
- **(C)** $1.44 \times 10^{-2} \mathrm{~J}$
- **(D)** $3.6 \times 10^{-2} \mathrm{~J}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) $1.44 \times 10^{-2} \mathrm{~J}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The work done in stretching a liquid film is given by the product of surface tension and the change in surface area:

$W = T \times \Delta A$

Since a liquid film has two free surfaces (front and back), the total increase in surface area is:

$\Delta A = 2 \times L \times \Delta x$

Given:
Length of the wire, $L = 10 \text{ cm} = 0.1 \text{ m}$
Increase in distance, $\Delta x = 1 \text{ mm} = 10^{-3} \text{ m}$
Surface tension, $T = 72 \text{ N/m}$

Substituting the values to find the change in area:

$\Delta A = 2 \times 0.1 \times 10^{-3} = 2 \times 10^{-4} \text{ m}^2$

Now, calculating the work done:

$W = 72 \times 2 \times 10^{-4}$

$W = 144 \times 10^{-4} \text{ J}$

$W = 1.44 \times 10^{-2} \text{ J}$

Answer: $1.44 \times 10^{-2} \mathrm{~J}$

</details>

---

### Q106. MHT CET 2024 (04 May Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

The excess pressure inside a spherical drop of water A is four times that of another drop B. Then the ratio of mass of drop $A$ to that of drop $B$ is

**Options:**

- **(A)** $1: 4$
- **(B)** $1: 8$
- **(C)** $1: 16$
- **(D)** $1: 64$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(D) $1: 64$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Excess pressure inside a spherical drop of water is given by $\Delta P = \dfrac{2T}{R}$.

Given $\Delta P_A = 4 \Delta P_B$.

$\dfrac{2T}{R_A} = 4 \left(\dfrac{2T}{R_B}\right)$

$R_B = 4 R_A$

The mass of a spherical drop is $M = \dfrac{4}{3} \pi R^3 \rho$.

The ratio of mass of drop A to that of drop B is

$\dfrac{M_A}{M_B} = \left(\dfrac{R_A}{R_B}\right)^3$

$\dfrac{M_A}{M_B} = \left(\dfrac{1}{4}\right)^3 = \dfrac{1}{64}$

Answer: $1: 64$

</details>

---

### Q107. MHT CET 2024 (04 May Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

Water rises up to height ' $X$ ' in a capillary tube immersed vertically in water. When the whole arrangement is taken to a depth ' $d$ ' in a mine, the water level rises up to height ' $Y$ '. If ' $R$ ' is
the radius of earth then the ratio $\dfrac{Y}{X}$ is

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\left(1-\dfrac{d}{R}\right)^{-1}$
- **(B)** $\left(1-\dfrac{d}{R}\right)$
- **(C)** $\left(1+\dfrac{d}{R}\right)^{-1}$
- **(D)** $\left(1+\dfrac{d}{R}\right)$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) $\left(1-\dfrac{d}{R}\right)^{-1}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The height of capillary rise is given by $h = \dfrac{2T \cos \theta}{\rho g r}$.

At the surface of the earth, the height is $X = \dfrac{2T \cos \theta}{\rho g r}$.

At a depth $d$ in a mine, the acceleration due to gravity is $g' = g \left(1 - \dfrac{d}{R}\right)$.

The height of capillary rise at this depth is $Y = \dfrac{2T \cos \theta}{\rho g' r}$.

Taking the ratio of $Y$ to $X$, we get:

$\dfrac{Y}{X} = \dfrac{g}{g'}$

Substituting the value of $g'$, we get:

$\dfrac{Y}{X} = \dfrac{g}{g \left(1 - \dfrac{d}{R}\right)} = \left(1 - \dfrac{d}{R}\right)^{-1}$

Answer: $\left(1-\dfrac{d}{R}\right)^{-1}$

</details>

---

### Q108. MHT CET 2024 (03 May Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `3`

A liquid drop of density ' $\rho$ ' is floating half immersed in a liquid of density ' $d$ '. If ' $T$ ' is the surface tension then the diameter of the liquid drop is ( $\mathrm{g}=$ acceleration due to gravity)

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\left[\dfrac{6 \mathrm{~T}}{\mathrm{~g}(2 \rho-\mathrm{d})}\right]^{\dfrac{1}{2}}$
- **(B)** $\left[\dfrac{8 \mathrm{~T}}{3 \mathrm{~g}(2 \rho-\mathrm{d})}\right]^{\dfrac{1}{2}}$
- **(C)** $\left[\dfrac{12 \mathrm{~T}}{\mathrm{~g}(2 \rho-\mathrm{d})}\right]^{\dfrac{1}{2}}$
- **(D)** $\left[\dfrac{3 \mathrm{~T}}{\mathrm{~g}(2 \rho-\mathrm{d})}\right]^{\dfrac{\mathrm{t}}{2}}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) $\left[\dfrac{12 \mathrm{~T}}{\mathrm{~g}(2 \rho-\mathrm{d})}\right]^{\dfrac{1}{2}}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Let $R$ be the radius of the liquid drop.

The weight of the drop acting downwards is:
$W = \dfrac{4}{3} \pi R^3 \rho g$

Since the drop is half immersed, the volume of the displaced liquid is $\dfrac{2}{3} \pi R^3$. The buoyant force (upthrust) acting upwards is:
$F_B = \dfrac{2}{3} \pi R^3 d g$

The surface tension force acts upwards along the circumference of the drop at the liquid surface. The length of the contact line is $2 \pi R$. The force due to surface tension is:
$F_T = 2 \pi R T$

For the drop to be in equilibrium, the total downward force must equal the total upward force:
$W = F_B + F_T$

$\dfrac{4}{3} \pi R^3 \rho g = \dfrac{2}{3} \pi R^3 d g + 2 \pi R T$

$\dfrac{2}{3} \pi R^3 (2 \rho - d) g = 2 \pi R T$

$R^2 = \dfrac{3 T}{g (2 \rho - d)}$

$R = \left[ \dfrac{3 T}{g (2 \rho - d)} \right]^{\dfrac{1}{2}}$

The diameter of the drop is $D = 2 R$:
$D = 2 \left[ \dfrac{3 T}{g (2 \rho - d)} \right]^{\dfrac{1}{2}} = \left[ \dfrac{12 T}{g (2 \rho - d)} \right]^{\dfrac{1}{2}}$

Answer: $\left[\dfrac{12 \mathrm{~T}}{\mathrm{~g}(2 \rho-\mathrm{d})}\right]^{\dfrac{1}{2}}$

</details>

---

### Q109. MHT CET 2024 (03 May Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

The pressure inside a soap bubble A is 1.01 atmosphere and that in a soap bubble B is 1.02 atmosphere. The ratio of volume of bubble A to that of $B$ is [Surrounding pressure $=1$ atmosphere $]$

**Options:**

- **(A)** $101: 102$
- **(B)** $102: 101$
- **(C)** $8: 1$
- **(D)** $2: 1$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) $8: 1$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The excess pressure inside a soap bubble is given by $\Delta P = \dfrac{4T}{R}$

For bubble A, the excess pressure is $\Delta P_A = 1.01 - 1 = 0.01$ atm

For bubble B, the excess pressure is $\Delta P_B = 1.02 - 1 = 0.02$ atm

Since $R = \dfrac{4T}{\Delta P}$, the ratio of their radii is

$\dfrac{R_A}{R_B} = \dfrac{\Delta P_B}{\Delta P_A} = \dfrac{0.02}{0.01} = 2$

The volume of a spherical bubble is $V = \dfrac{4}{3}\pi R^3$

The ratio of their volumes is

$\dfrac{V_A}{V_B} = \left(\dfrac{R_A}{R_B}\right)^3 = (2)^3 = 8$

The ratio of the volume of bubble A to that of B is $8:1$

Answer: $8: 1$

</details>

---

### Q110. MHT CET 2024 (03 May Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

The pressure inside two soap bubbles, (A) is 1.01 and that of (B) is 1.02 atmosphere respectively. The ratio of their respective radii (A to B) is (outside pressure $=1 \mathrm{~atm}$.)

**Options:**

- **(A)** $2: 1$
- **(B)** $4: 1$
- **(C)** $6: 1$
- **(D)** $8: 1$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) $2: 1$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The excess pressure inside a soap bubble is given by $\Delta P = \dfrac{4T}{R}$.

For bubble A, the excess pressure is $\Delta P_A = P_A - P_0 = 1.01 - 1 = 0.01 \text{ atm}$.

For bubble B, the excess pressure is $\Delta P_B = P_B - P_0 = 1.02 - 1 = 0.02 \text{ atm}$.

Since the radius $R$ is inversely proportional to the excess pressure $\Delta P$, the ratio of their radii is:

$\dfrac{R_A}{R_B} = \dfrac{\Delta P_B}{\Delta P_A} = \dfrac{0.02}{0.01} = \dfrac{2}{1}$

The ratio of their respective radii is $2:1$.

Answer: $2: 1$

</details>

### Q111. MHT CET 2024 (02 May Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

Liquid drops are falling slowly one by one from vertical glass tube. The relation between the weight of a drop ' $w$ ', the surface tension ' $T$ ' and the radius ' $r$ ' of the bore of the tube is (Angle of contact is zero)

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\mathrm{W}=\pi \mathrm{r}^2 \mathrm{~T}$
- **(B)** $\mathrm{W}=2 \pi^2 \mathrm{r} T$
- **(C)** $\quad \mathrm{W}=\left(\dfrac{4}{2}\right) \pi^2 \mathrm{rT}$
- **(D)** $\quad \mathrm{W}=2 \pi \mathrm{rT}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(D) $\quad \mathrm{W}=2 \pi \mathrm{rT}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

When a liquid drop falls slowly from a vertical glass tube, it detaches when its weight is just balanced by the upward force due to surface tension.

The surface tension acts along the circumference of the bore of the tube.

The circumference of the bore is $2 \pi r$.

The upward force due to surface tension is given by $F = T \times 2 \pi r \times \cos(\theta)$.

Given that the angle of contact $\theta$ is zero, $\cos(0) = 1$.

Equating the weight of the drop to the surface tension force, we get $W = 2 \pi r T$.

Answer: $\quad \mathrm{W}=2 \pi \mathrm{rT}$

</details>

### Q112. MHT CET 2024 (02 May Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `3`

The pressure inside a soap bubble A is 1.01 atmosphere and that in a soap bubble B is 1.02 atmosphere. The ratio of volume of A to that of $B$ is

**Options:**

- **(A)** $2: 1$
- **(B)** $8: 1$
- **(C)** $101: 102$
- **(D)** $102: 101$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) $8: 1$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Excess pressure inside a soap bubble is given by $\Delta P = \dfrac{4T}{R}$

Let the atmospheric pressure be $P_0 = 1$ atm.

For bubble A, excess pressure $\Delta P_A = 1.01 - 1 = 0.01$ atm.

For bubble B, excess pressure $\Delta P_B = 1.02 - 1 = 0.02$ atm.

Since $\Delta P \propto \dfrac{1}{R}$, we have $R \propto \dfrac{1}{\Delta P}$

The volume of a bubble is $V = \dfrac{4}{3}\pi R^3$, so $V \propto \dfrac{1}{(\Delta P)^3}$

The ratio of volumes is $\dfrac{V_A}{V_B} = \left(\dfrac{\Delta P_B}{\Delta P_A}\right)^3 = \left(\dfrac{0.02}{0.01}\right)^3 = 2^3 = 8$

Thus, the ratio of volume of A to that of B is $8:1$.

Answer: $8: 1$

</details>

### Q114. MHT CET 2023 (14 May Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

1000 small water drops of equal size combine to form a big drop. The ratio of final surface energy to the total initial surface energy is

**Options:**

- **(A)** $10:1$
- **(B)** $1:10$
- **(C)** $1000:1$
- **(D)** $1:1000$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) $1:10$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Radii of the bigger and smaller drops are related as, $\mathrm{R} \propto \mathrm{n}^{\frac{1}{3}} \mathrm{r}$
Now, surface energy $E=T d A=T \times 4 \pi R^2$
$\begin{array}{ll}
\therefore \quad & \frac{\mathrm{E}_2}{\mathrm{E}_1}=\frac{4 \pi \mathrm{R}^2 \times \mathrm{T}}{\mathrm{n} \times 4 \pi \mathrm{r}^2 \mathrm{~T}} \\
\therefore \quad \frac{\mathrm{E}_2}{\mathrm{E}_1} & =\frac{\mathrm{R}^2}{\mathrm{nr}^2}=\frac{\mathrm{n}^{\frac{2}{3}} \times \mathrm{r}^2}{\mathrm{nr}^2} \\
& \frac{\mathrm{E}_2}{\mathrm{E}_1}=\frac{1}{\mathrm{n}^{\frac{1}{3}}}=\frac{1}{1000^{\frac{1}{3}}} \\
\therefore \quad \frac{\mathrm{E}_2}{\mathrm{E}_1} & =1: 10
\end{array}$

</details>

### Q115. MHT CET 2023 (14 May Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

Two spherical soap bubbles of radii ' $a$ ' and ' $b$ ' in vacuum coalesce under isothermal conditions. The resulting bubble has a radius equal to

**Options:**

- **(A)** $a+b$
- **(B)** $\frac {a+b}{2}$
- **(C)** $\sqrt{a^2+b^2}$
- **(D)** $\frac {a+b}{ab}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) $\sqrt{a^2+b^2}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Number of moles is conserved, so
$\mathrm{P}_1 \mathrm{~V}_1+\mathrm{P}_2 \mathrm{~V}_2=\mathrm{P}_3 \mathrm{~V}$
But, $\mathrm{P}=\frac{4 \mathrm{~T}}{\mathrm{r}}$ where, $\mathrm{r}$ is the radius of the bubble
$\begin{aligned}
& \therefore \quad \frac{4 \mathrm{~T}}{\mathrm{a}}\left(\frac{4}{3} \pi \mathrm{a}^3\right)+\frac{4 \mathrm{~T}}{\mathrm{~b}}\left(\frac{4}{3} \pi \mathrm{b}^3\right)=\frac{4 \mathrm{~T}}{\mathrm{c}}\left(\frac{4}{3} \pi \mathrm{c}^3\right) \\
& \mathrm{a}^2+\mathrm{b}^2=\mathrm{c}^2 \\
& \mathrm{c}=\sqrt{\mathrm{a}^2+\mathrm{b}^2}
\end{aligned}$

</details>

### Q116. MHT CET 2023 (14 May Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

Water rises in a capillary tube of radius ' $r$ ' upto a height ' $h$ '. The mass of water in a capillary is ' $m$ '. The mass of water that will rise in a capillary tube of radius $\frac{\text { ' } r \text { ' }}{3}$ will be

**Options:**

- **(A)** $3 \mathrm{~m}$
- **(B)** $\frac{\mathrm{m}}{3}$
- **(C)** $\mathrm{m}$
- **(D)** $\frac{2 \mathrm{~m}}{3}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) $\frac{\mathrm{m}}{3}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

$\begin{array}{ll} & \mathrm{h} \propto \frac{1}{\mathrm{r}} \\ & \text { Mass of water in a capillary, } \mathrm{m}=\pi \mathrm{r}^2 \mathrm{~h} \rho \\ \therefore \quad & \mathrm{m} \propto \mathrm{r}^2 \mathrm{~h} \\ \therefore \quad & \mathrm{m} \propto \mathrm{r}^2 / \mathrm{r} \\ \therefore \quad & \frac{\mathrm{m}_2}{\mathrm{~m}}=\frac{\frac{\mathrm{r}}{3}}{\mathrm{r}} \\ \therefore \quad & \mathrm{m}_2=\frac{\mathrm{m}}{3}\end{array}$

</details>

### Q117. MHT CET 2023 (13 May Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

If work done in blowing a soap bubble of volume ' $\mathrm{V}$ ' is $\mathrm{W}$ then the work done in blowing the bubble of volume $2 \mathrm{~V}$ from same soap solution is

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\frac{\mathrm{W}}{2}$
- **(B)** $\sqrt{2} \mathrm{~W}$
- **(C)** $(2)^{\frac{1}{3}} \mathrm{~W}$
- **(D)** $(4)^{\frac{1}{3}} \mathrm{~W}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(D) $(4)^{\frac{1}{3}} \mathrm{~W}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The work done is given as $\mathrm{W}=\mathrm{T} \Delta \mathrm{A}$
Volume of sphere is $\mathrm{V}=\frac{4}{3} \pi \mathrm{r}^3$
Area of sphere is $\mathrm{A}=4 \pi \mathrm{r}^2$
$\therefore \quad \mathrm{A} \propto \mathrm{V}^{\frac{2}{3}}$
$\therefore \quad \mathrm{W} \propto \mathrm{V}^{\frac{2}{3}}$
$\frac{\mathrm{W}^{\prime}}{\mathrm{W}}=\frac{\mathrm{V}^{\frac{2}{3}}}{\mathrm{~V}^{\frac{2}{3}}}$
$\frac{\mathrm{W}^{\prime}}{\mathrm{W}}=\frac{(2 \mathrm{~V})^{\frac{2}{3}}}{\mathrm{~V}^{\frac{2}{3}}} \quad \ldots .(\because \mathrm{V}=2 \mathrm{~V})$
$\therefore \quad \frac{\mathrm{W}^{\prime}}{\mathrm{W}}=2^{\frac{2}{3}}=4^{\frac{1}{3}}$
$\therefore \quad \mathrm{W}^{\prime}=4^{\frac{1}{3}} \mathrm{~W}$

</details>

### Q118. MHT CET 2023 (13 May Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

Twenty seven droplets of water each of radius $0.1 \mathrm{~mm}$ merge to form a single drop then the energy released is

**Options:**

- **(A)** $1.6 \times 10^{-3} \mathrm{~J}$
- **(B)** $1.6 \mathrm{~J}$
- **(C)** $1600 \mathrm{~J}$
- **(D)** $1.6 \times 10^{-7} \mathrm{~J}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(D) $1.6 \times 10^{-7} \mathrm{~J}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

From
$$
\begin{aligned}
& \mathrm{W}=4 \pi \mathrm{r}^2 \mathrm{~T}\left(\mathrm{n}-\mathrm{n}^{2 / 3}\right) \\
& \mathrm{W}=4 \pi \times\left(0.1 \times 10^{-3}\right)^2 \times 0.072\left[27-(3)^{2 / 3}\right] \\
& =1.627 \times 10^{-7} \quad\left(\mathrm{~T}_{\text {water }}=0.075 \mathrm{~N} / \mathrm{m}\right)
\end{aligned}
$$

</details>

### Q119. MHT CET 2023 (13 May Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

A large number of water droplets each of radius ' $t$ ' combine to form a large drop of Radius ' $R$ '. If the surface tension of water is ' $T$ ' \& mechanical equivalent of heat is ' $\mathrm{J}$ ' then the rise in temperature due to this is

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\frac{2 \mathrm{~T}}{\mathrm{rJ}}$
- **(B)** $\frac{3 \mathrm{~T}}{\mathrm{RJ}}$
- **(C)** $\frac{3 \mathrm{~T}}{\mathrm{~J}}\left(\frac{1}{\mathrm{r}}-\frac{1}{\mathrm{R}}\right)$
- **(D)** $\frac{2 \mathrm{~T}}{\mathrm{~J}}\left(\frac{1}{\mathrm{r}}-\frac{1}{\mathrm{R}}\right)$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) $\frac{3 \mathrm{~T}}{\mathrm{~J}}\left(\frac{1}{\mathrm{r}}-\frac{1}{\mathrm{R}}\right)$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Radius of each droplet $=\mathrm{r}$
Radius of the drop $=\mathrm{R}$
As volume remains constant,
$$
\begin{aligned}
& \mathrm{n} \times \frac{4}{3} \pi \mathrm{r}^3=\frac{4}{3} \pi \mathrm{R}^3 \\
& \therefore \quad \mathrm{n}=\frac{\mathrm{R}^3}{\mathrm{r}^3}
\end{aligned}
$$
Decrease in surface area $=4 \pi r^2 n-4 \pi R^2 n$
$$
\begin{aligned}
\Delta \mathrm{A} & =4 \pi\left[\mathrm{nr}^2-\mathrm{R}^2\right] \\
& =4 \pi\left[\frac{\mathrm{R}^3}{\mathrm{r}^3} \mathrm{r}^2-\mathrm{R}^2\right] \\
& =4 \pi \mathrm{R}^3\left[\frac{1}{\mathrm{r}}-\frac{1}{\mathrm{R}}\right]
\end{aligned}
$$
Enery released $\mathrm{W}=\mathrm{T} \times \Delta \mathrm{T}$
$$
\begin{aligned}
& \text { Heat produced } \mathrm{Q}=\frac{\mathrm{W}}{\mathrm{J}} \\
& \text { H }
\end{aligned}
$$
Heat produced $\mathrm{Q}=\frac{\mathrm{W}}{\mathrm{J}}$
$$
\mathrm{Q}=\mathrm{m} \cdot \mathrm{s} \cdot \Delta \theta
$$
Put (i) and (iii) into (ii)
$$
\begin{aligned}
& \mathrm{m} \cdot \mathrm{S} \Delta \theta=\frac{4 \pi \mathrm{R}^3 \mathrm{~T}}{\mathrm{~J}}\left[\frac{1}{\mathrm{r}}-\frac{1}{\mathrm{R}}\right] \\
& \frac{4}{3} \pi \mathrm{R} \rho_{\text {water }} \mathrm{S}_{\text {water }} \Delta \theta=\frac{4 \pi \mathrm{R}^3 \mathrm{~T}}{\mathrm{~J}}\left[\frac{1}{\mathrm{r}}-\frac{1}{\mathrm{R}}\right] \\
& \Delta \mathrm{Q}=\frac{3 \mathrm{~T}}{\mathrm{~J}}\left[\frac{1}{\mathrm{r}}-\frac{1}{\mathrm{R}}\right]
\end{aligned}
$$

</details>

### Q120. MHT CET 2023 (12 May Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

Eight small drops of mercury each of radius ' $r$ ', coalesce to form a large single drop. The ratio of total surface energy before and after the change is

**Options:**

- **(A)** $2: 1$
- **(B)** $1: 1$
- **(C)** $1: 4$
- **(D)** $1: 8$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) $2: 1$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Let $\mathrm{R}$ be the radius of the coalesced drop.
$$
\begin{aligned}
\therefore \quad & \frac{4}{3} \pi R^3=8 \times \frac{4}{3} \pi r^3 \\
& R^3=8 \pi r^3 \\
& R=2 r
\end{aligned}
$$

Surface Energy $\mathrm{E}=\mathrm{T}$.dA
$$
\begin{aligned}
& E_1=8 \times T \times d A=8 \times T \times 4 \pi r^2 \\
& E_2=T \times d A=T \times 4 \pi R^2
\end{aligned}
$$

Where $E_1$ and $E_2$ are the surface energies before and after coalescing.

Dividing equation (i) by (ii),
$$
\begin{aligned}
& \frac{\mathrm{E}_1}{\mathrm{E}_2}=\frac{8 \mathrm{r}^2}{\mathrm{R}^2} \\
& \text { but } \mathrm{R}=2 \mathrm{r} \\
& \therefore \quad \frac{\mathrm{E}_1}{\mathrm{E}_2}=\frac{8 \mathrm{r}^2}{4 \mathrm{r}^2}=\frac{2}{1} \\
&
\end{aligned}
$$

</details>

### Q121. MHT CET 2023 (12 May Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

The excess pressure inside a first spherical drop of water is three times that of second spherical drop of water. Then the ratio of mass of first spherical drop to that of second spherical drop is

**Options:**

- **(A)** $1: 3$
- **(B)** $1: 6$
- **(C)** $1: 9$
- **(D)** $1: 27$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(D) $1: 27$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Excess pressure inside the $1^{\text {st }}$ spherical drop is given by,
$$
\begin{aligned}
\mathrm{P}_1 & =\frac{2 \mathrm{~T}}{\mathrm{r}_1} \\
& \text { Similarly, for } 2^{\text {nd }} \text { drop } \\
\mathrm{P}_2 & =\frac{2 \mathrm{~T}}{\mathrm{r}_2} \\
\mathrm{P}_1 & =3 \mathrm{P}_2 .......(Given)\\
\therefore \quad \frac{2 \mathrm{~T}}{\mathrm{r}_1} & =3\left(\frac{2 \mathrm{~T}}{\mathrm{r}_2}\right) \\
\therefore \quad \frac{\mathrm{r}_1}{\mathrm{r}_2} & =\frac{1}{3}
\end{aligned}
$$
Now, $\frac{m_1}{m_2}=\frac{V_1 \rho_1}{V_2 \rho_2}$
As both the drops are of water, $\rho_1=\rho_2$
$$
\begin{aligned}
& \therefore \quad \frac{\mathrm{m}_1}{\mathrm{~m}_2}=\frac{\mathrm{V}_1}{\mathrm{~V}_2} \\
& \therefore \quad \frac{\mathrm{m}_1}{\mathrm{~m}_2}=\frac{4 / 3 \pi_1^3}{4 / 3 \mathrm{r}_2^3} \\
& \therefore \quad \frac{\mathrm{m}_1}{\mathrm{~m}_2}=\frac{1}{27}
\end{aligned}
$$

</details>

### Q122. MHT CET 2023 (12 May Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

A spherical drop of liquid splits into 1000 identical spherical drops. If ' $\mathrm{E}_1$ ' is the surface energy of the original drop and ' $\mathrm{E}_2$ ' is the total surface energy of the resulting drops, then $\frac{E_1}{E_2}=\frac{x}{10}$. Then value of ' $x$ ' is

**Options:**

- **(A)** 9
- **(B)** 7
- **(C)** 3
- **(D)** 1

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(D) 1**

**Detailed Solution:**

$\begin{array}{ll} & r=\text { Radius of small drop } \\ & R=\text { Radius of bigger drop } \\ \therefore \quad & \frac{4}{3} \pi R^3=\frac{4}{3} \pi(1000) r^3 \\ \therefore \quad & R=10 r \\ & E_1=T_1^2=T\left(4 \pi R^2\right) \\ & E_2=n A_2^2=1000 \times T\left(4 \pi r^2\right) \\ \therefore \quad & \frac{E_1}{E_2}=\frac{R^2}{1000 r^2}=\frac{(10 r)^2}{1000 r^2} \\ \therefore \quad & \frac{E_1}{E_2}=\frac{1}{10} \\ \therefore \quad & x=1\end{array}$

</details>

### Q123. MHT CET 2023 (12 May Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

Consider a soap film on a rectangular frame of wire of area $3 \times 3 \mathrm{~cm}^2$. If the area of the soap film is increased to $5 \times 5 \mathrm{~cm}^2$, the work done in the process will be (surface tension of soap solution is $2.5 \times 10^{-2} \mathrm{~N} / \mathrm{m}$ )

**Options:**

- **(A)** $9 \times 10^{-6} \mathrm{~J}$
- **(B)** $16 \times 10^{-6} \mathrm{~J}$
- **(C)** $40 \times 10^{-6} \mathrm{~J}$
- **(D)** $80 \times 10^{-6} \mathrm{~J}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(D) $80 \times 10^{-6} \mathrm{~J}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

$$
\begin{aligned}
& A_1=9 \times 10^{-4} \mathrm{~m}^2, A_2=25 \times 10^{-4} \mathrm{~m}^2 \\
& T=2.5 \times 10^{-2} \mathrm{~N} / \mathrm{m}
\end{aligned}
$$
Work done,
$$
\begin{aligned}
& W=2 T \Delta A=2 \times 2.5 \times 10^{-2} \times(25-9) \times 10^{-4} \\
& W=80 \times 10^{-6} \mathrm{~J}
\end{aligned}
$$

</details>
