### Q124. MHT CET 2023 (11 May Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

A liquid drop of radius ' $R$ ' is broken into ' $n$ ' identical small droplets. The work done is $[\mathrm{T}=$ surface tension of the liquid]

**Options:**

- **(A)** $4 \pi R^2\left(n^{\frac{2}{3}}-1\right) T$
- **(B)** $4 \pi R^2\left(n^{\frac{1}{3}}-1\right) T$
- **(C)** $4 \pi R^2\left(1-n^{\frac{1}{3}}\right) T$
- **(D)** $4 \pi R^2\left(1-n^{\frac{2}{3}}\right) T$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) $4 \pi R^2\left(n^{\frac{1}{3}}-1\right) T$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Volume of $\mathrm{n}$ smaller droplets $=$ Volume of bigger drop
$\begin{aligned}
& \mathrm{n} \frac{4}{3} \pi \mathrm{r}^3=\frac{4}{3} \pi \mathrm{R}^3 \\
& \therefore \quad \mathrm{R}=\mathrm{n}^{\frac{1}{3}} \cdot \mathrm{r} \\
& \mathrm{r}=\frac{\mathrm{R}}{\mathrm{n}^{\frac{1}{3}}}
\end{aligned}$
$\begin{aligned} \text { Work done } W & =\left[n \cdot 4 \pi r^2-4 \pi R^2\right] T \\ & =4 \pi\left[n \cdot \frac{R^2}{n^{\frac{2}{3}}}-R^2\right] T \\ & =4 \pi R^2\left[n^{\frac{1}{3}}-1\right] T\end{aligned}$

</details>

### Q125. MHT CET 2023 (11 May Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

A spherical liquid drop of radius $\mathrm{R}$ is divided into 8 equal droplets. If surface tension is $\mathrm{S}$, then the work done in this process will be

**Options:**

- **(A)** $2 \pi R^2 S$
- **(B)** $3 \pi R^2 S$
- **(C)** $4 \pi R^2 S$
- **(D)** $2 \pi \mathrm{RS}^2$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) $4 \pi R^2 S$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Work done, $\mathrm{W}=\mathrm{S} \times \Delta \mathrm{A}$
Where, $\Delta \mathrm{A}$ is a change in surface area. Since, the radius of the big drop be $\mathrm{R}$ $\mathrm{A}_{\text {initial }}=4 \pi \mathrm{R}^2$

Let the radius of the small drops be $\mathrm{r}$ $\mathrm{A}_{\text {final }}=8 \times 4 \pi \mathrm{r}^2$

The volume is constant
$$
\begin{array}{ll}
\therefore & \frac{4}{3} \pi \mathrm{R}^3=8 \times \frac{4}{3} \pi \mathrm{r}^3 \\
\therefore & \mathrm{R}^3=8 \mathrm{r}^3 \\
\therefore & \mathrm{R}=2 \mathrm{r}
\end{array}
$$

Substituting the values in the formula.
$$
\begin{aligned}
& \mathrm{W}=\mathrm{S} \times \Delta \mathrm{A} \\
& \mathrm{W}=\mathrm{S}\left(\mathrm{A}_{\text {final }}-\mathrm{A}_{\text {initial }}\right)
\end{aligned}
$$
$\begin{aligned} & \mathrm{W}=\mathrm{S}\left(8 \times 4 \pi \mathrm{r}^2-4 \pi \mathrm{R}^2\right) \\ & \mathrm{W}=\mathrm{S}\left(32 \pi\left(\frac{\mathrm{R}}{2}\right)^2-4 \pi \mathrm{R}^2\right) \\ & \mathrm{W}=\mathrm{S}\left(8 \pi \mathrm{R}^2-4 \pi \mathrm{R}^2\right) \\ & \mathrm{W}=4 \pi \mathrm{R}^2 \mathrm{~S}\end{aligned}$

</details>

---

### Q126. MHT CET 2023 (11 May Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

Two capillary tubes of the same diameter are kept vertically in two different liquids whose densities are in the ratio $4: 3$. The rise of liquid in two capillaries is ' $h_1$ ' and ' $h h_2$ ' respectively. If the surface tensions of liquids are in the ratio $6: 5$, the ratio of heights $\left(\frac{h_1}{h_2}\right)$ is
(Assume that their angles of contact are same)

**Options:**

- **(A)** $0.4$
- **(B)** $0.5$
- **(C)** $0.8$
- **(D)** $0.9$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(D) $0.9$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Given: Density ratio: $\frac{\rho_1}{\rho_2}=\frac{4}{3}$ and surface tension ratio: $\frac{\mathrm{T}_1}{\mathrm{~T}_2}=\frac{6}{5}$
$\therefore \quad$ Rise of liquid in a capillary:
$$
\mathrm{h}=\frac{2 \mathrm{~T} \cos \theta}{\mathrm{r} \rho \mathrm{g}}
$$

For constant $\theta, \mathrm{r}$ and $\mathrm{g}$,
$$
\begin{aligned}
\mathrm{h} & \propto \frac{\mathrm{T}}{\rho} \\
\therefore \quad & \frac{\mathrm{h}_1}{\mathrm{~h}_2}=\frac{\mathrm{T}_1 \rho_2}{\mathrm{~T}_2 \rho_1}=\frac{6 \times 3}{5 \times 4}=0.9
\end{aligned}
$$

</details>

### Q128. MHT CET 2023 (10 May Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

Three liquids have same surface tension and densities $\rho_1, \rho_2$, and $\rho_3\left(\rho_1>\rho_2>\rho_3\right)$. In three identical capillaries rise of liquid is same. The corresponding angles of contact $\theta_1, \theta_2$ and $\theta_3$ are related as

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\theta_1>\theta_2>\theta_3$
- **(B)** $\theta_1>\theta_3>\theta_2$
- **(C)** $\theta_1 < \theta_2 < \theta_3$
- **(D)** $\theta_1=\theta_2=\theta_3$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) $\theta_1 < \theta_2 < \theta_3$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Rise in capillary tube,
$\mathrm{h}=\frac{2 \mathrm{~T} \cos \theta}{\mathrm{rpg}}$
Given that, $\mathrm{h}, \mathrm{T}, \mathrm{r}$ and $\mathrm{g}$ are constant.
$\begin{array}{ll}
\therefore \quad & \frac{\cos \theta}{\rho}=\text { constant } \\
& \text { i.e., } \frac{\cos \theta_1}{\rho_1}=\frac{\cos \theta_2}{\rho_2}=\frac{\cos \theta_3}{\rho_3} \\
& \text { as } \rho_1>\rho_2>\rho_3 \\
& \cos \theta_1>\cos \theta_2>\cos \theta_3 \\
\therefore \quad & \theta_1 < \theta_2 < \theta_3
\end{array}$

</details>

### Q129. MHT CET 2023 (09 May Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `3`

A film of soap solution is formed between two straight parallel wires of length $10 \mathrm{~cm}$ each separated by $0.5 \mathrm{~cm}$. If their separation is increased by $1 \mathrm{~mm}$ while still maintaining their parallelism. How much work will have to be done?
(surface tension of solution $=65 \times 10^{-2} \mathrm{~N} / \mathrm{m}$ )

**Options:**

- **(A)** $7.22 \times 10^{-6} \mathrm{~J}$
- **(B)** $13.0 \times 10^{-5} \mathrm{~J}$
- **(C)** $2.88 \times 10^{-5} \mathrm{~J}$
- **(D)** $5.76 \times 10^{-5} \mathrm{~J}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) $13.0 \times 10^{-5} \mathrm{~J}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The increase in surface area of the film is, $\Delta \mathrm{A}=\mathrm{A}_2-\mathrm{A}_1$
$\mathrm{A}_1=2 \times l \times \mathrm{b}=2 \times 10 \times 10^{-2} \times 0.5 \times 10^{-2}$
$\mathrm{A}_2=2 \times l \times(\mathrm{b}+1)=2 \times 10 \times 10^{-2} \times(0.5+0.1)$
$\times 10^{-2}$
$A_2-A_1=\left[2 \times 10 \times 10^{-2} \times(0.5+0.1) \times 10^{-2}\right]$
$-\left[2 \times 10 \times 10^{-2} \times 0.5 \times 10^{-2}\right]$
$=2 \times 10^{-4} \mathrm{~m}^2$
$\therefore \quad$ Work done $=$ Increase in surface energy
$=\mathrm{TdA}$
$=\left(65 \times 10^{-2}\right) \times\left(2 \times 10^{-4}\right)$
$=1.3 \times 10^{-4}$
$=13 \times 10^{-5} \mathrm{~J}$

</details>

---

### Q130. MHT CET 2023 (09 May Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

The excess of pressure in a first soap bubble is three times that of other soap bubble. Then the ratio of the volume of first bubble to other is

**Options:**

- **(A)** $1:3$
- **(B)** $1:9$
- **(C)** $27:1$
- **(D)** $1:27$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(D) $1:27$**

**Detailed Solution:**

$\begin{aligned} & \quad \Delta \mathrm{P} \propto \frac{1}{\mathrm{r}} \Rightarrow \frac{\mathrm{r}_1}{\mathrm{r}_2}=\frac{\Delta \mathrm{P}_2}{\Delta \mathrm{P}_1}=\frac{1}{3} \\ & \therefore \quad \frac{\mathrm{V}_1}{\mathrm{~V}_2}=\left(\frac{\mathrm{r}_1}{\mathrm{r}_2}\right)^3=\frac{1}{27}\end{aligned}$

</details>

### Q131. MHT CET 2022 (11 Aug Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

The radii of two mercury drops are $R_1$ and $R_2$. Under isothermal conditions, a single drop of radius $R$ is formed from them. The relations between $R, R_1$ and $R_2$ is

**Options:**

- **(A)** $R^2=R_1^2+R_2^2$
- **(B)** $R=R_1+R_2$
- **(C)** $R=\frac{R_1+R_2}{2}$
- **(D)** $R^3=R_1^3+R_2^3$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(D) $R^3=R_1^3+R_2^3$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Total volume remains same,
$\begin{aligned}
& \frac{4}{3} \pi R^3=\frac{4}{3} \pi R_1^3+\frac{4}{3} \pi R_2^3 \\
& R^3=R_1^3+R_2^3
\end{aligned}$

</details>

### Q132. MHT CET 2022 (11 Aug Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

The work done in blowing a soap bubble of radius $R$ is $W_1$ and that of radius $2 R$ is $W_2$. The ratio of $W_1$ to $W_2$ is

**Options:**

- **(A)** 1:4
- **(B)** 4:1
- **(C)** 2:1
- **(D)** 1:2

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) 1:4**

**Detailed Solution:**

$\begin{aligned} & W_1=2\left[4 \pi r^2 T\right] \\ & W_2=2\left[4 \pi(2 r)^2 T\right] \\ & \Rightarrow \frac{W_1}{W_2}=\frac{1}{4}\end{aligned}$

</details>

### Q133. MHT CET 2022 (10 Aug Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `3`

The work done in blowing a soap bubble of radius $R$ is $W_1$ at room temperature. Now the soap solution is heated. From the heated solution another soap bubble of radius $2 R$ is blown and the work done is $W_2$. Then

**Options:**

- **(A)** $W_2=0$
- **(B)** $W_2=4 W_1$
- **(C)** $W_2<4 W_1$
- **(D)** $W_2=W_1$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) $W_2<4 W_1$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The work done in forming a soap in forming a soap bubble of radius $r$ is $W_1=8 \pi r^2 T_1$, where $T_1$ is the surface tension at room temperature and the work done to form a soap bubble of radius $2 r$ is
$\therefore \frac{W_2}{W_1}=\frac{328 \pi r^2 T_2}{8 \pi r^2 T_1}=\frac{4 T_2}{T_1}$
If $T_1=T_2$, then $W_2=4 W_1$
But it is given that the solution is heated. As the temperature is increased, the surface tension of the soap solution is decreases
$\therefore T_2<T_1 \quad \therefore W_2<4 W_1$

</details>

### Q134. MHT CET 2022 (10 Aug Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

The excess pressure inside a soap bubble of radius $2 \mathrm{~cm}$ is 50 dyne $/ \mathrm{cm}^2$. The surface tension is

**Options:**

- **(A)** 25 dyne $/ \mathrm{cm}$
- **(B)** 60 dyne/ cm
- **(C)** 50 dyne $/ \mathrm{cm}$
- **(D)** 75 dyne/ cm

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) 25 dyne $/ \mathrm{cm}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Given,
Excess pressure, $\Delta P=50$ dyne $/ \mathrm{cm}^2$
Radius, $r=2 \mathrm{~cm}$
For a soap bubble liquid-air interface is crossed two times, so we have,
Excess pressure, $\Delta P=\frac{4 T}{r}$, where $\mathrm{T}$ is the surface tension.
$\therefore T=\frac{\Delta P \times r}{4}=\frac{50 \mathrm{dyne} / \mathrm{cm}^2 \times 2 \mathrm{~cm}}{4}=25 \mathrm{dyne} / \mathrm{cm}$

</details>

### Q135. MHT CET 2022 (10 Aug Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

A steel coin of thickness $d$ and density $\rho$ is floating on water of surface tension $T$. The radius of the coin $R$ is [ $g$ = acceleration due to gravity]

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\frac{4 T}{3 \rho g d}$
- **(B)** $\frac{T}{\rho g d}$
- **(C)** $\frac{2 T}{\rho g d}$
- **(D)** $\frac{3 T}{4 \rho g d}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) $\frac{2 T}{\rho g d}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Considering, contact angle is zero, and liquid loves the surface. The weight of the coin is balanced by surface tension.
$\begin{aligned} & F=T(2 \pi R)=\rho\left(d \pi R^2\right) g \\ & \Rightarrow R=\frac{2 T}{\rho g d}\end{aligned}$

</details>

A square wire frame of each side $L$ is dipped in soap solution. On taking out, a membrane is formed. If the surface tension of solution is $T$, the force acting on the frame will be

### Q137. MHT CET 2022 (10 Aug Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `3`

Water rises up to a height $h$ in a capillary tube immersed vertically in water. When this whole arrangement is taken to a depth $d$ in a mine, the water level rises up to a height $h^{\prime}$. If $R$ is the radius of the earth, then the ratio $\frac{h}{h^{\prime}}$ is

**Options:**

- **(A)** $1+\frac{d}{R}$
- **(B)** $1-\frac{d}{R}$
- **(C)** $\frac{R+d}{R-d}$
- **(D)** $\frac{R-d}{R+d}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) $1-\frac{d}{R}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

On the surface of the earth $T=\frac{r h \rho g}{2}$ for water
$\therefore h=\frac{2 T}{r \rho g}$
At a depth $d$, the value of acceleration due to gravity changes to
$g^{\prime}=g\left(1-\frac{d}{R}\right)$, therefore
$g^{\prime}=g\left(1-\frac{d}{R}\right)$, therefore
$$
\begin{aligned}
& h^{\prime}=\frac{2 T}{r \rho g^{\prime} d}=\frac{2 T}{r \rho g\left(1-\frac{d}{R}\right)} \\
& \therefore \frac{h}{h^{\prime}}=\frac{2 T}{r \rho g} \times \frac{r \rho g\left(1-\frac{d}{R}\right)}{2 T}=1-\frac{d}{R}
\end{aligned}
$$

</details>

### Q138. MHT CET 2022 (08 Aug Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `3`

Due to surface tension, the excess pressure inside a smaller drop is 9 units. If 27 smaller drops of same liquid combine then the excess pressure inside the bigger drop is

**Options:**

- **(A)** 18 units
- **(B)** 9 units
- **(C)** 6 units
- **(D)** 3 units

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(D) 3 units**

**Detailed Solution:**

<img src="https://cdn-question-pool.getmarks.app/pyq/mht_cet/C-2mx4VbouzCAIcgL0OwXqVZJ4d4vrk4QeHfyBAkf3I.original.fullsize.png">

Excess pressure Or Pressure jump $=2\left(\frac{\sigma}{r}\right)=9$ units
If 27 such drops combine, total volume is: $\mathrm{V}_{\mathrm{B}}=27 \mathrm{~V}_{\mathrm{S}}$
Using volume conservation:
$\begin{aligned}
& \frac{4}{3} \pi \mathrm{R}^3=27\left(\frac{4}{3} \pi \mathrm{R}^3\right) \\
& \Rightarrow \mathrm{R}=(3 \mathrm{r}) \\
& \therefore \text { Excess pressure }=\frac{2 \sigma}{\mathrm{R}}=\frac{2 \sigma}{3 \mathrm{r}}=3 \text { units }
\end{aligned}$

</details>

### Q139. MHT CET 2022 (08 Aug Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `3`

Two glass plates of area $10^{-2} \mathrm{~m}^2$ have $10 \mathrm{~cm}$ thick water film between them. The surface tension of water is $70 \times 10^{-3} \mathrm{~N} / \mathrm{m}$. The force required to separate two glass plates from each other is

**Options:**

- **(A)** 14 N
- **(B)** 17 N
- **(C)** 28 N
- **(D)** 30 N

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) 14 N**

**Detailed Solution:**

Consider complete wetting.
<img src="https://cdn-question-pool.getmarks.app/pyq/mht_cet/GnVKDK1Z-nZFJPBZwpgmEsaZJlN6hxVcKcypfklZQR4.original.fullsize.png">

The Laplace pressure jump is given by:
$\begin{aligned}
& \Delta P=\frac{\sigma}{\left(\frac{h}{2}\right)}=\frac{F}{A} \\
& \Rightarrow F=\frac{A \sigma}{\frac{h}{2}}=\frac{10^{-2} \times 70 \times 10^{-3}}{5 \times 10^{-2}} N=28 \mathrm{~N}
\end{aligned}$

</details>

### Q140. MHT CET 2022 (08 Aug Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `3`

A certain number of spherical liquid drops of radius $r$ coalesce to form a single drop of radius $R$ and volume $V$. If $T$ is the surface tension of the liquid, which one of the following statements is true for the energy $(E)$ in the process?

**Options:**

- **(A)** $E=3 V T\left[\frac{1}{r}-\frac{1}{R}\right]$ is absorbed.
- **(B)** $E=4 V T\left[\frac{1}{r}-\frac{1}{R}\right]$ is released.
- **(C)** $E=3 V T\left[\frac{1}{r}-\frac{1}{R}\right]$ is released.
- **(D)** $E=4 V T\left[\frac{1}{r}-\frac{1}{R}\right]$ is absorbed.

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) $E=3 V T\left[\frac{1}{r}-\frac{1}{R}\right]$ is released.**

**Detailed Solution:**

Change in surface energy is given by
$E=T(\Delta A) \quad---(1)$
The initial area is given by,
$\mathrm{A}=\left(4 \pi r^2\right) n$
The final area is given by,
$\mathrm{a}=4 \pi R^2$
Therefore, the change in area is given by,
$\begin{aligned}
& \Delta A=a-A \\
& \Rightarrow \Delta A=4 \pi\left(n r^2-R^2\right)---(2)
\end{aligned}$
Now, using volume conservation: $\left(\frac{4}{3} \pi r^2\right) n=\frac{4}{3} R^3$
$\therefore n=\frac{R^3}{r^3} \quad---(3)$
$\Delta A=4 \pi\left[\frac{R^3}{r^3} \cdot r^2-R^2\right]=4 \pi\left[\frac{R^3}{r}-\frac{R^3}{R}\right]=\left(\frac{4}{3} \pi R^3\right) 3\left[\frac{1}{r}-\frac{1}{R}\right]=3 V\left[\frac{1}{r}-\frac{1}{R}\right]$
Introducing above value in equation (1)
$E=3 V T\left[\frac{1}{r}-\frac{1}{R}\right]$

</details>

---

### Q141. MHT CET 2022 (07 Aug Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

The amount of work done in blowing a sops bubble such that its diameter increases from ' $d$ ' to $\mathrm{D}$ ' is ( $\mathrm{T}$ = surface tension of solution )

**Options:**

- **(A)** $4 \pi\left(D^2-d^2\right) T$
- **(B)** $8 \pi\left(D^2-d^2\right) T$
- **(C)** $\pi\left(D^2-d^2\right) T$
- **(D)** $2 \pi\left(D^2-d^2\right) T$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(D) $2 \pi\left(D^2-d^2\right) T$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Change in surface area
$\begin{aligned} & =2 \times 4 \pi\left[\left(\frac{\mathrm{D}}{2}\right)^2-\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}}{2}\right)^2\right]=2 \pi\left(\mathrm{D}^2-\mathrm{d}^2\right) \\ & \therefore \text { Work done }=\text { surface tension } \times \text { change in area } \\ & =2 \pi\left(\mathrm{D}^2-\mathrm{d}^2\right) \mathrm{T}\end{aligned}$

</details>

### Q142. MHT CET 2022 (07 Aug Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

Water rises in a capillary tube of radius ' $r$ ' upto height ' $h$ ' The mass of water in the capillary is ' $m$ ' The mass of water will rise in a capillary of radius will $\frac{r}{4}$ be

**Options:**

- **(A)** $4 \mathrm{~m}$
- **(B)** $\frac{\mathrm{m}}{4}$
- **(C)** $\mathrm{m}$
- **(D)** $\frac{4}{\mathrm{~m}}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) $\frac{\mathrm{m}}{4}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The height of water column in a capillary tube is given by
$\mathrm{h}=\frac{2 \mathrm{~T} \cos \theta}{\mathrm{perg}}$
<img src="https://cdn-question-pool.getmarks.app/pyq/mht_cet/0yEijc0JxXPTTmiOyHS3zj_y30W8LjtNo4xHhurbKDM.original.fullsize.png">
From eqs. (i) and (ii), we get
$\begin{aligned} & m \propto r^2 \times \frac{1}{r} \\ & m \propto r \\ & \Rightarrow \frac{m^{\prime}}{m}=\frac{\frac{r}{4}}{r} \Rightarrow m^{\prime}=\frac{m}{4}\end{aligned}$

</details>

### Q143. MHT CET 2022 (07 Aug Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

In capillary tube having area of cross-section ' $\mathrm{A}$ ', water rises to a height ' $h$ '. If cross sectional area is reduced to $\frac{A}{9}$, the rise of water in the capillary tube is

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\mathrm{h}$
- **(B)** $4 h$
- **(C)** $3 \mathrm{~h}$
- **(D)** $2 \mathrm{~h}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) $3 \mathrm{~h}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

$\begin{aligned} & \mathrm{h}=\frac{2 \mathrm{~T} \cos \theta}{\mathrm{r \rho g}} \\ & \Rightarrow \mathrm{h} \propto \frac{1}{\mathrm{r}}\end{aligned}$
And $\mathrm{A}=\pi \mathrm{r}^2 \Rightarrow \mathrm{r} \propto \sqrt{\mathrm{A}}$
$\Rightarrow \mathrm{h} \propto \frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathrm{A}}}$
Now, $\frac{h_2}{h_1}=\sqrt{\frac{A_1}{A_2}}$
$\begin{aligned} & \Rightarrow \frac{h_2}{h}=\sqrt{\frac{A}{A / 9}}=3 \\ & \Rightarrow h_2=3 h\end{aligned}$

</details>

### Q144. MHT CET 2022 (07 Aug Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

A square frame of each side ' $L$ ' is dipped in a soap solution and taken out. The force acting on the film formed is $(\mathrm{T}=$ surface tension of soap solution)

<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

Soap film has two surfaces.
Net force acting on frame $=2$ (force on one surface)

Net force acting on the square frame $=8 \mathrm{TL}$

### Q145. MHT CET 2022 (07 Aug Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `3`

A water drop of radius ' $r$ ' and volume ' $\mathrm{V}$ ' is kept in between the two identical glass plates such that it forms a thin layer of area ' $\mathrm{A}$ ' between the plates. A force ' $\mathrm{F}$ ' is applied such that the two plates separate from each other. The surface tension ' $\mathrm{T}$ ' of the liquid is

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\frac{F V}{2 A^2}$
- **(B)** $\frac{\mathrm{A}^2}{\mathrm{FV}}$

<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

### Q146. MHT CET 2022 (06 Aug Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `3`

Two narrow tube of diameters $d_1$ and $d_2$ are joined together to form a Utube open at both ends. If U-tube contains water, the difference in water levels in the limbs is
( $T$ is the surface tension of water, angle of contact is zero and density of water is $\rho, g$ is the acceleration due to gravity)

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\frac{4 T}{\rho g}\left[\frac{d_2-d_1}{d_1 d_2}\right]$
- **(B)** $\frac{4 T}{\rho g}\left[\frac{d_1 d_2}{d_1+d_2}\right]$
- **(C)** $\frac{2 T}{\rho g}\left[\frac{d_2-d_1}{d_1 d_2}\right]$
- **(D)** $\frac{2 T}{\rho g}\left[\frac{d_1+d_2}{d_1 d_2}\right]$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) $\frac{4 T}{\rho g}\left[\frac{d_2-d_1}{d_1 d_2}\right]$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Consider the situation below:
<img src="https://cdn-question-pool.getmarks.app/pyq/mht_cet/6AyWgicTzu6JfNn2hf6eObMGMB26XVYwgytiNn3Gnh4.original.fullsize.png">
When angle of contact is zero degree, the radius of meniscus equals the radius of tube.
Excess pressure in the first tube $P_1=\frac{4 T}{d_1}$
Excess pressure in the second tube, $P_2=\frac{4 T}{d_2}$
Hence, the pressure difference in the two limbs of the tube is given by,
$\Delta P=P_1-P_2=h \rho g$
$\Rightarrow h=\frac{P_1-P_2}{\rho g}=\frac{4 T}{\rho g}\left(\frac{1}{d_1}-\frac{1}{d_2}\right)=\frac{4 T}{\rho g}\left[\frac{d_2-d_1}{d_1 d_2}\right]$

</details>

### Q147. MHT CET 2022 (06 Aug Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `3`

Two soap bubbles of radii $r_1$ and $r_2$ in vacuum coalesce under isothermal conditions. The resulting bubble has a radius equal to

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\sqrt{r_1^2+r_2^2}$
- **(B)** $\frac{r_1+r_2}{2}$
- **(C)** $r_1+r_2$
- **(D)** $\frac{r_1 r_2}{r_1+r_2}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) $\sqrt{r_1^2+r_2^2}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

In Iso-thermal process: $p V=\mathrm{C}$. The pressure inside bubble is: $p \propto 1 / r$ and volume is: $V \propto r^3$.
$\therefore p V \propto r^2$
And $n=\frac{p V}{R T}$, therefore number of moles of gas inside the bubble is proportional to the square of its radius
$\therefore n \propto r^2$
On combining bubbles, the total number of moles is conserved: Therefore, $n_1+n_1=N$
$\therefore r_1^2+r_2^2=R^2$
The above result can also be obtained by total surface energy conservation:
$2 \times\left(4 \pi R^2\right) \sigma=2 \times\left(4 \pi r_1^2\right) \sigma+2 \times\left(4 \pi r_2^2\right) \sigma$
or $R=\sqrt{r_1^2+r_2^2}$

</details>

---

### Q148. MHT CET 2022 (06 Aug Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

If the excess pressure inside a soap bubble of radius $3 \mathrm{~mm}$ is equal to the pressure of a water column of height $0.8 \mathrm{~cm}$, then the surface tension of the soap solution is ( $\rho_{\text {water }}=1000 \mathrm{~kg} / \mathrm{m}^3, g=9.8 \mathrm{~m} / \mathrm{s}^2$ )

**Options:**

- **(A)** $0.588 \times 10^{-3} \mathrm{~N} / \mathrm{m}$
- **(B)** $588 \times 10^{-3} \mathrm{~N} / \mathrm{m}$
- **(C)** $58.8 \times 10^{-3} \mathrm{~N} / \mathrm{m}$
- **(D)** $5.88 \times 10^{-3} \mathrm{~N} / \mathrm{m}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) $58.8 \times 10^{-3} \mathrm{~N} / \mathrm{m}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The excess pressure inside the bubble is given as, $P=\frac{4 T}{r}$ as the soap bubble has two liquid-gas interfaces.
The pressure to height column of water is given as, $P=\rho h g$
Since the excess pressure inside the bubble is balanced by the height of the column and it can be written as,
$\frac{4 T}{r}=\rho h g$
$\Rightarrow T=\frac{r \rho h g}{4}$
On plugging the given values:
$T=\frac{1}{4}\left(3 \times 10^{-3} \mathrm{~m} \times \frac{10^3 \mathrm{~kg}}{\mathrm{~m}^3} \times 8 \times 10^{-3} \mathrm{~m} \times 9.8 \frac{\mathrm{m}}{\mathrm{s}^2}\right)=58.8 \times 10^{-3} \mathrm{~N} / \mathrm{m}$

</details>

---

### Q149. MHT CET 2022 (05 Aug Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

If a capillary tube of radius $1 \mathrm{~mm}$ is immersed in water, the mass of water rising in the capillary tube is $\mathrm{m}$. If the radius of the capillary tube is doubled, then the mass of water that rises in the same capillary tube will be

**Options:**

- **(A)** $3 \mathrm{~m}$
- **(B)** $\mathrm{m} / 2$
- **(C)** $\mathrm{m}$
- **(D)** $2 \mathrm{~m}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(D) $2 \mathrm{~m}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

For capillary rise, $h_1 r_1=h_2 r_2$
$\therefore \frac{h_2}{h_1}=\frac{r_1}{r_2}=\frac{1}{2}$
And $m=\pi r^2 h_1 \rho$ and $M=p(2 r)^2 \times h_2 \times \rho$
$\begin{aligned} & \therefore \frac{M^{\prime}}{M}=\left(\frac{2 r}{r}\right)^2 \times\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)=4 \times \frac{1}{2}=2 \\ & \therefore M=2 \mathrm{~m}\end{aligned}$

</details>

---

### Q150. MHT CET 2022 (05 Aug Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

The surface tension of a soap solution is ' $\mathrm{T}$ '. Work done in blowing a soap bubble of diameter $2 \mathrm{~d}$ is

**Options:**

- **(A)** $8 \pi d^2 T$
- **(B)** $2 \pi d^2 T$
- **(C)** $4 \pi d^2 T$
- **(D)** $6 \pi d^2 T$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) $8 \pi d^2 T$**

**Detailed Solution:**

In this case, work done = increase in surface energy, i.e.
$\mathrm{W}=\mathrm{T} \Delta \mathrm{A}$
Key point: In calculation, area of sphere have been multiplied with 2, since there are two surfaces in soap bubble.
$\begin{aligned} & \therefore \mathrm{W}=\mathrm{T} \times\left[2\left(4 \pi \mathrm{d}^2\right)\right] \\ & \Rightarrow \mathrm{W}=8 \pi \mathrm{d}^2 \mathrm{~T}\end{aligned}$

</details>

---

### Q151. MHT CET 2022 (05 Aug Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

A liquid drop having surface energy ' $E$ ' is spread into 216 droplets of the same size. The final surface energy of the droplets is

**Options:**

- **(A)** $3 E$
- **(B)** $8 \mathrm{E}$
- **(C)** $2 E$
- **(D)** $6 \mathrm{E}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(D) $6 \mathrm{E}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The correct option is (D).
Concept: Surface Energy is proportional to the surface area $\mathrm{E} \propto 4 \pi R^2$.
Considering volume conservation, the size of small droplets $r$ is:
$V=\frac{4}{3} \pi R^3=216 \times \frac{4}{3} \pi r^3$
Therefore, $\mathrm{R}=6 \mathrm{r}$
The total surface area of the new 216 droplets is:
$216 \times\left(4 \pi r^2\right)$
or $6 \times\left(4 \pi R^2\right)$
Given $E \propto 4 \pi R^2$
Therefore, the total surface energy of the new droplets would be $6 \mathrm{E}$

</details>

### Q152. MHT CET 2022 (05 Aug Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

Which one of the following statements is 'NOT' True about the angle of contact of a liquid?

**Options:**

- **(A)** Any increase in the temperature of the liquid does not decrease its angle of contact.
- **(B)** If an impurity is added in the liquid then it's angle of contact changes.
- **(C)** Angle of contact depends upon the nature of liquid and solid in contact.
- **(D)** At a given temperature, the angle of contact is constant for a solid-liquid surface.

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) Any increase in the temperature of the liquid does not decrease its angle of contact.**

**Detailed Solution:**

The correct option is (A).
Concept: Contact angle decreases with the increasing temperature.
Addition of impurity decreases the surface tension of liquid.
Contact angle depends on the nature of liquid and solid in contact.
Therefore, answer (A) is the only "NOT" true statement.
Concept based question.

</details>

### Q153. MHT CET 2021 (24 Sep Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

If the work done in blowing a soap bubble of volume ' $\mathrm{V}$ ' is ' $\mathrm{W}$ ', then the work done in blowing a soap bubble of volume ' $2 \mathrm{~V}$ ' will be

**Options:**

- **(A)** $2 \mathrm{~W}$
- **(B)** $4^{1 / 3} \mathrm{~W}$
- **(C)** W
- **(D)** $\sqrt{2} \mathrm{~W}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) $4^{1 / 3} \mathrm{~W}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

If $\mathrm{V}$ is the volume then we have
$\begin{aligned}
& \mathrm{V}=\frac{4}{3} \pi \mathrm{r}^3 \\
& \mathrm{~V} \propto \mathrm{r}^3 \\
& \therefore \frac{\mathrm{V}_2}{\mathrm{~V}_1}=\left(\frac{\mathrm{r}_2}{\mathrm{r}_1}\right)^3 \\
& \frac{\mathrm{r}_2}{\mathrm{r}_1}=\left(\frac{\mathrm{V}_2}{\mathrm{~V}_1}\right)^{1 / 3}=(2)^{1 / 3} \\
& \mathrm{~W}_1=8 \pi \mathrm{r}_1^2 \cdot \mathrm{T} \text { and } \mathrm{W}_2=8 \pi \mathrm{r}_2^2 \mathrm{~T} \\
& \therefore \frac{\mathrm{W}_2}{\mathrm{~W}_1}=\left(\frac{\mathrm{r}_2}{\mathrm{r}_1}\right)^2=(2)^{2 / 3}=(4)^{1 / 3} \\
& \mathrm{~W}_2=(4)^{1 / 3} \mathrm{~W}
\end{aligned}$

</details>

---

### Q154. MHT CET 2021 (24 Sep Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

Pressure inside two soap bubbles is $1.01 \mathrm{~atm}$ and $1.03 \mathrm{~atm}$. The ratio between their volumes is (Pressure outside the soap bubble is $1 \mathrm{~atm}$ )

**Options:**

- **(A)** 9:1
- **(B)** 27:1
- **(C)** 81:1
- **(D)** 3:1

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) 27:1**

**Detailed Solution:**

Excess pressure inside a soap bubble is given by
$\Delta \mathrm{P}=\mathrm{P}_{\mathrm{i}}-\mathrm{P}_0=\frac{4 \mathrm{~T}}{\mathrm{r}}$
$\begin{aligned} & \Delta \mathrm{P}_1=1.01 \mathrm{~atm}-1 \mathrm{~atm}=0.01 \mathrm{~atm} \\ & \Delta \mathrm{P}_2=1.03 \mathrm{~atm}-1 \mathrm{~atm}=0.03 \mathrm{~atm}\end{aligned}$
$\begin{aligned} & \therefore \frac{\Delta \mathrm{P}_1}{\Delta \mathrm{P}_2}=\frac{\mathrm{r}_2}{\mathrm{r}_1} \\ & \frac{0.03}{0.01}=3=\frac{\mathrm{r}_2}{\mathrm{r}_1} \\ & \frac{\mathrm{V}_2}{\mathrm{~V}_1}=\left(\frac{\mathrm{r}_2}{\mathrm{r}_1}\right)^3=(3)^3=27\end{aligned}$

</details>

---

### Q155. MHT CET 2021 (24 Sep Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

An ice cube of edge $1 \mathrm{~cm}$ melts in a gravity free container. The approximate surface area of water formed is (water is in the form of spherical drop)

**Options:**

- **(A)** $(36 \pi)^{1 / 3} \mathrm{~cm}^2$
- **(B)** $(24 \pi)^{1 / 3} \mathrm{~cm}^2$
- **(C)** $(28 \pi)^{1 / 3} \mathrm{~cm}^2$
- **(D)** $(12 \pi)^{1 / 3} \mathrm{~cm}^2$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) $(36 \pi)^{1 / 3} \mathrm{~cm}^2$**

**Detailed Solution:**

$$
\mathrm{x}=1 \mathrm{~cm}
$$
$\therefore$ Volume of the cube $\mathrm{v}=\mathrm{x}^3=1 \mathrm{~cm}^3$, volume of drop $=$ volume of cube.
$$
\begin{aligned}
& \frac{4}{3} \pi r^3=x^3=1 \mathrm{~cm}^3 \\
& \therefore r^3=\frac{3}{4 \pi} \text { or } r=\left(\frac{3}{4 \pi}\right)^{\frac{1}{3}} \\
& \therefore r^2=\left(\frac{9}{16 \pi^2}\right)^{\frac{1}{3}}
\end{aligned}
$$
Surface area of drop $=4 \pi r^2=4 \pi\left(\frac{9}{16 \pi^2}\right)^{\frac{1}{3}}$
$$
=\left(64 \pi^3 \times \frac{9}{16 \pi^2}\right)^{\frac{1}{3}}=(36 \pi)^{\frac{1}{3}}
$$

</details>

---

### Q156. MHT CET 2021 (24 Sep Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `3`

A glass rod of radius ' $r$ ' ' is inserted symmetrically into a vertical capillary tube of radius ' $\mathrm{r}_2$ ' $\left(\mathrm{r}_1 < \mathrm{r}_2\right)$ such that their lower ends are at same level. The arrangement is dipped in water. The height to which water will rise into the tube will be ( $\rho=$ density of water, $\mathrm{T}=$ surface tension in water, $\mathrm{g}$ = acceleration due to gravity)

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\frac{2 \mathrm{~T}}{\left(\mathrm{r}_2-\mathrm{r}_1\right) \rho g}$
- **(B)** $\frac{\mathrm{T}}{\left(\mathrm{r}_2^2-\mathrm{r}_1^2\right) \rho g}$
- **(C)** $\frac{\mathrm{T}}{\left(\mathrm{r}_2-\mathrm{r}_1\right) \rho \mathrm{g}}$
- **(D)** $\frac{2 \mathrm{~T}}{\left(\mathrm{r}_2^2-\mathrm{r}_1^2\right) \rho g}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) $\frac{2 \mathrm{~T}}{\left(\mathrm{r}_2-\mathrm{r}_1\right) \rho g}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Vertical component of total force of surface tension
$$
\mathrm{F}=\left(\mathrm{r}_2+\mathrm{r}_1\right) 2 \pi \mathrm{T} \cos \theta
$$
Weight of the liquid in the capillary
$$
\mathrm{W}=\pi\left(\mathrm{r}_2^2-\mathrm{r}_1^2\right) \mathrm{h} \rho \mathrm{g}
$$
This is balanced by the vertical component of the face due to the surface tension
$$
\therefore \pi\left(\mathrm{r}_2^2-\mathrm{r}_1^2\right) \mathrm{h} \rho \mathrm{g}=\left(\mathrm{r}_2+\mathrm{r}_1\right) \times 2 \pi \mathrm{T} \cos \theta
$$
Simplifying and solving for $\mathrm{h}$ we get,
$$
\mathrm{h}=\frac{2 \mathrm{~T} \cos \theta}{\left(\mathrm{r}_2-\mathrm{r}_1\right) \rho g}=\frac{2 \mathrm{~T}}{\left(\mathrm{r}_2-\mathrm{r}_1\right) \rho g}
$$
(For pure water, $\theta=0^{\circ}, \cos \theta=1$ )

</details>

---

### Q157. MHT CET 2021 (24 Sep Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

Water rises up to a height of $4 \mathrm{~cm}$ in a capillary tube. The lower end of the capillary tube is at a depth of $8 \mathrm{~cm}$ below the water level. The mouth pressure required to blow an air bubble at the lower end of the capillary will be ' $\mathrm{X}$ ' $\mathrm{cm}$ of water, where $\mathrm{X}$ is equal to

**Options:**

- **(A)** 10
- **(B)** 8
- **(C)** 6
- **(D)** 12

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(D) 12**

**Detailed Solution:**

At the lower ends of the tube, the total pressure is due to height of water column in the container plus the height of water in the capillary.
$\therefore$ Total pressure is due to $8+4=12 \mathrm{~cm}$ of water.

</details>

### Q158. MHT CET 2021 (23 Sep Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

Work done in increasing the size of a soap bubble from radius of $3 \mathrm{~cm}$ to $5 \mathrm{~cm}$ in millijoule is nearly (surface tension of soap solution $=0.03 \mathrm{Nm}^{-1}$ )

**Options:**

- **(A)** $0.4 \pi$
- **(B)** $0.2 \pi$
- **(C)** $4 \pi$
- **(D)** $2 \pi$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) $0.4 \pi$**

**Detailed Solution:**

$\begin{aligned} & \mathrm{w}=8 \pi \mathrm{T}\left(\mathrm{r}_2^2-\mathrm{r}_1^2\right) \\ & =8 \pi \times 0.03(25-9) \times 10^{-4} \\ & =0.384 \pi \times 10^{-3} \mathrm{~J} \\ & =0.384 \pi \mathrm{mJ} \approx 0.4 \pi \mathrm{mJ}\end{aligned}$

</details>

---

### Q159. MHT CET 2021 (23 Sep Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

Water rises to a height of $2 \mathrm{~cm}$ in a capillary tube. If crosssectional area of the tube is reduced to $\frac{1}{16}^{\text {th }}$ of initial area, then water will rise to a height of

**Options:**

- **(A)** $4 \mathrm{~cm}$
- **(B)** $8 \mathrm{~cm}$
- **(C)** $12 \mathrm{~cm}$
- **(D)** $16 \mathrm{~cm}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) $8 \mathrm{~cm}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

$\begin{aligned} & \mathrm{h}=\frac{2 \mathrm{~T} \cos \theta}{\mathrm{r} \rho \mathrm{g}} \\ & \therefore \frac{\mathrm{h}_2}{\mathrm{~h}_1}=\frac{\mathrm{r}_1}{\mathrm{r}_2} \\ & \text { Area } \mathrm{A}=\pi \mathrm{r}^2 \therefore \mathrm{A} \propto \mathrm{r}^2 \\ & \therefore \frac{\mathrm{A}_1}{\mathrm{~A}_2}=\frac{\mathrm{r}_1^2}{\mathrm{r}_2^2} \text { or } \frac{\mathrm{r}_1}{\mathrm{r}_2}=\sqrt{\frac{\mathrm{A}_1}{\mathrm{~A}_2}}=\sqrt{16} \\ & \frac{\mathrm{r}_1}{\mathrm{r}_2}=4 \\ & \therefore \frac{\mathrm{h}_2}{\mathrm{~h}_1}=4 \text { or } \mathrm{h}_2=4 \mathrm{~h}_1=4 \times 2=8 \mathrm{~cm}\end{aligned}$

</details>

---

### Q160. MHT CET 2021 (23 Sep Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `3`

A thin metal disc of radius ' $r$ ' floats on water surface and bends the surface downwards along the perimeter making an angle ' $\theta$ ' with the vertical edge of the disc. If the weight of water displaced by the disc is $\mathrm{W}$, the weight of the metal disc is [ $\mathrm{T}=$ surface tension of water $]$

**Options:**

- **(A)** $2 \pi \mathrm{r} \cos \theta+\mathrm{W}$
- **(B)** $\mathrm{W}-2 \pi \mathrm{rT} \cos \theta$
- **(C)** $2 \pi r \mathrm{~T}+\mathrm{W}$
- **(D)** $2 \pi \mathrm{rT} \cos \theta-\mathrm{W}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) $2 \pi \mathrm{r} \cos \theta+\mathrm{W}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The weight of the disc is balanced by the force due to the surface tension and the upthrust of water.
The component of surface tension in vertically upward direction is $\mathrm{T} \cos \theta$ and the force acting due to it $2 \pi \mathrm{r} \mathrm{T} \cos \theta$.
The upthrust is equal to the weight of the water displaced (W).
$\therefore$ Weight of the disc $=2 \pi \mathrm{rT} \cos \theta+\mathrm{W}$

</details>

---

### Q161. MHT CET 2021 (23 Sep Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

The work done in blowing a soap bubble of volume ' $\mathrm{V}$ ' is ' $\mathrm{W}$ '. The work required to blow a soap bubble of volume ' $2 \mathrm{~V}$ ' is [ $\mathrm{T}=$ surface tension of soap solution $]$

**Options:**

- **(A)** $2^{2 / 3} \mathrm{~W}$
- **(B)** 2W
- **(C)** W
- **(D)** $2^{1 / 2} \mathrm{~W}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) $2^{2 / 3} \mathrm{~W}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

$\begin{aligned} & \mathrm{V}=\frac{4}{3} \pi \mathrm{r}^3 \text { or } \quad \mathrm{r}=\left(\frac{3 \mathrm{~V}}{4 \pi}\right)^{1 / 3} \\ & \mathrm{~W}=8 \pi \mathrm{r}^2 \mathrm{~T}=8 \pi\left(\frac{3 \mathrm{~V}}{4 \pi}\right)^{2 / 3} \mathrm{~T} \\ & \mathrm{~W} \propto \mathrm{V}^{2 / 3} \\ & \frac{\mathrm{W}^{\prime}}{\mathrm{W}}=\left(\frac{2 \mathrm{~V}}{\mathrm{~V}}\right)^{2 / 3}=2^{2 / 3}\end{aligned}$

</details>

### Q162. MHT CET 2021 (22 Sep Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

A needle is $7 \mathrm{~cm}$ long. Assuming that the needle is not wetted by water, what is the weight of the needle, so that floats on water?
$$
\left[\mathrm{T}=\text { surface tension of water }=70 \frac{\mathrm{dyne}}{\mathrm{cm}}\right]
$$
[acceleration due to gravity $=980 \mathrm{~cm} \mathrm{~s}^{-2}$ ]

**Options:**

- **(A)** 1 g wt
- **(B)** 5 g wt
- **(C)** 3 g wt
- **(D)** 7 g wt

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) 1 g wt**

**Detailed Solution:**

The weight of the needle is balanced by the force due to surface tension
$$
\begin{aligned}
& \mathrm{W}=2 \mathrm{TL} \\
& =2 \times 70 \times 7 \\
& =980 \mathrm{dyne} \\
& =1 \mathrm{~g} \mathrm{wt}
\end{aligned}
$$

</details>

### Q163. MHT CET 2021 (22 Sep Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

Let ' $W_1$ ' be the work done in blowing a soap bubble of radius ' $r$ ' from soap solution at room temperature. The soap solution is now heated and second soap bubble of radius ' $2 \mathrm{r}$ ' is blown from the heated soap solution. If ' $\mathrm{W}_2$ ' is the work done in forming this bubble then

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\mathrm{W}_2=2 \mathrm{~W}_1$
- **(B)** $\mathrm{W}_2=4 \mathrm{~W}_1$
- **(C)** $\mathrm{W}_2>4 \mathrm{~W}_1$
- **(D)** $\mathrm{W}_2 < 4 \mathrm{~W}_1$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(D) $\mathrm{W}_2 < 4 \mathrm{~W}_1$**

**Detailed Solution:**

$$
\mathrm{W}_1=8 \pi \mathrm{r}^2 \mathrm{~T}
$$
Where, $\mathrm{T}=$ surface tension
$$
\mathrm{W}_2=8 \pi(2 \mathrm{r})^2 \mathrm{~T}^{\prime}=32 \pi \mathrm{r}^2 \mathrm{~T}^{\prime}
$$
Since soap solution is heated, its surface tension decrease.
$$
\begin{aligned}
& \therefore \mathrm{T}^{\prime} < \mathrm{T} \\
& \therefore \mathrm{W}_2 < 4 \mathrm{~W}_1
\end{aligned}
$$

</details>

---

### Q164. MHT CET 2021 (22 Sep Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `3`

A drop of liquid of density ' $\rho$ ' is floating half immersed in a liquid of density ' $d$ '. If ' $T$ ' is the surface tension, then the diameter of the drop of the liquids is

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\sqrt{\frac{6 T}{g(2 \rho-d)}}$
- **(B)** $\sqrt{\frac{T}{g(2 \rho-d)}}$
- **(C)** $\sqrt{\frac{2 T}{g(2 \rho-d)}}$
- **(D)** $\sqrt{\frac{12 T}{g(2 \rho-d)}}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(D) $\sqrt{\frac{12 T}{g(2 \rho-d)}}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The drop is in equilibrium under the action of the following forces:
Weight of the liquid, $\mathrm{W}=\mathrm{Mg}=\frac{4}{3} \pi \mathrm{r}^3 \rho \mathrm{g}$ (downwards)
Upthrust $=$ weight of the liquid displaced
$$
\therefore \mathrm{F}_{\mathrm{t}}=\frac{2}{3} \pi \mathrm{r}^3 \mathrm{dg}
$$
(upwards)
Force due to surface tension, $\mathrm{F}=2 \pi \mathrm{rT}$ (upwards)
$$
\begin{aligned}
& \therefore \mathrm{Mg}=\mathrm{F}+\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm{t}} \\
& \therefore \mathrm{F}=\mathrm{Mg}-\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm{t}} \\
& \therefore 2 \pi \mathrm{r} T=\frac{4}{3} \pi \mathrm{r}^3 \rho g-\frac{2}{3} \pi \mathrm{r}^3 \mathrm{dg} \\
& \therefore \mathrm{T}=\frac{2}{3} \mathrm{r}^2 \rho g-\frac{1}{3} \mathrm{r}^2 \mathrm{dg} \\
& =\mathrm{r}^2 g\left(\frac{2}{3} \rho-\frac{1}{3} d\right) \\
& =\mathrm{r}^2 g\left(\frac{2 \rho-d}{3}\right) \\
& \therefore \mathrm{r}=\sqrt{\frac{3 \mathrm{~T}}{\mathrm{~g}(2 \rho-\mathrm{d})}} \\
& \therefore \mathrm{D}=\sqrt{\frac{12 \mathrm{~T}}{\mathrm{~g}(2 \rho-d)}}
\end{aligned}
$$

</details>

---

### Q165. MHT CET 2021 (22 Sep Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

Water rises in a capillary tube of radius ' $r$ ' to a height ' $h$ '. The mass of water in a capillary is ' $m$ '. The mass of water will rise in a capillary tube of radius $\frac{r}{3}$ will be

**Options:**

- **(A)** $3m$
- **(B)** $\frac{\mathrm{m}}{3}$
- **(C)** $m$
- **(D)** $\frac{2 \mathrm{~m}}{3}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) $\frac{\mathrm{m}}{3}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

$\begin{aligned} & \mathrm{h} \propto \frac{1}{\mathrm{r}} \quad \therefore \frac{\mathrm{h}_2}{\mathrm{~h}_1}=\frac{\mathrm{r}_1}{\mathrm{r}_2}=3 \\ & \therefore \mathrm{h}_2=3 \mathrm{~h}_1 \\ & \mathrm{~m}_1=\mathrm{h}_1 \pi \mathrm{r}_1^2 \rho, \mathrm{m}_2=\mathrm{h}_2 \pi \mathrm{r}_2^2 \rho \\ & \therefore \frac{\mathrm{m}_2}{\mathrm{~m}_1}=\frac{\mathrm{h}_2}{\mathrm{~h}_1}\left(\frac{\mathrm{r}_2}{\mathrm{r}_1}\right)^2=3 \times\left(\frac{1}{3}\right)^2=\frac{1}{3} \\ & \therefore \mathrm{m}_2=\frac{\mathrm{m}_1}{3}\end{aligned}$

</details>

---

### Q166. MHT CET 2021 (21 Sep Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

In capillary tube having area of cross-section ' $A$ ', water rises to a height ' $h$ '. If cross sectional area is reduced to $\frac{A}{9}$, the rise of water in the capillary tube is

**Options:**

- **(A)** h
- **(B)** 4 h
- **(C)** 3 h
- **(D)** 2h

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) 3 h**

**Detailed Solution:**

$\begin{aligned} & h=\frac{2 T \cos \theta}{r \rho g} \\ & \Rightarrow h \propto \frac{1}{r} \\ & \text { And } A=\pi r^2 \Rightarrow r \propto \sqrt{A} \\ & \Rightarrow h \propto \frac{1}{\sqrt{A}} \\ & \text { Now, } \frac{h_2}{h_1}=\sqrt{\frac{A_1}{A_2}} \\ & \Rightarrow \frac{h_2}{h}=\sqrt{\frac{A}{A / 9}}=3 \\ & \Rightarrow h_2=3 h\end{aligned}$

</details>

---

### Q167. MHT CET 2021 (21 Sep Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

A big water drop is divided into 8 equal droplets. $\Delta \mathrm{P}_{\mathrm{S}}$ and $\Delta \mathrm{P}_{\mathrm{B}}$ be the excess pressure inside a smaller and bigger drop respectively. The relation between $\Delta \mathrm{P}_{\mathrm{S}}$ and $\Delta \mathrm{P}_{\mathrm{B}}$ is

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\Delta \mathrm{P}_{\mathrm{B}}=\Delta \mathrm{P}_{\mathrm{S}}$
- **(B)** $\Delta \mathrm{P}_{\mathrm{B}}=\frac{1}{2} \Delta \mathrm{P}_{\mathrm{S}}$
- **(C)** $\Delta \mathrm{P}_{\mathrm{B}}=\frac{1}{4} \Delta \mathrm{P}_{\mathrm{S}}$
- **(D)** $\Delta \mathrm{P}_{\mathrm{B}}=2 \Delta \mathrm{P}_{\mathrm{S}}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) $\Delta \mathrm{P}_{\mathrm{B}}=\frac{1}{2} \Delta \mathrm{P}_{\mathrm{S}}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Volume of 8 smaller drop $=$ Volume of the bigger drop
$$
\begin{aligned}
& \therefore 8 \times \frac{4}{3} \pi r^3=\frac{4 \pi}{3} R^3 \\
& \therefore 2 r=R \text { or } r=\frac{R}{2}
\end{aligned}
$$
Excess pressure $\Delta \mathrm{P}_{\mathrm{s}}=\frac{2 \mathrm{~T}}{\mathrm{r}}$ and $\Delta \mathrm{P}_{\mathrm{B}}=\frac{2 \mathrm{~T}}{\mathrm{R}}$
$$
\therefore \frac{\Delta \mathrm{P}_{\mathrm{B}}}{\Delta \mathrm{P}_{\mathrm{S}}}=\frac{\mathrm{r}}{\mathrm{R}}=\frac{1}{2}
$$

</details>

---

### Q168. MHT CET 2021 (21 Sep Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

Water rises to a height $10 \mathrm{~cm}$ in a capillary tube. It will rise to a height which is much more than $10 \mathrm{~cm}$ in a very long capillary tube if the apparatus is kept.

**Options:**

- **(A)** on the surface of moon
- **(B)** at the north pole
- **(C)** in a lift moving up with an acceleration
- **(D)** on the equator

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) on the surface of moon**

**Detailed Solution:**

Rise in a capillary tube is given by
$$
\begin{aligned}
& \mathrm{h}=\frac{2 \mathrm{~T} \cos \theta}{\mathrm{r} \rho \mathrm{g}} \\
& \therefore \mathrm{h} \propto \frac{1}{\mathrm{~g}}
\end{aligned}
$$
Smaller the value of $g$. greater will be h. Out of the given options, value $g$ will be minimum for option (A)

</details>

### Q169. MHT CET 2021 (21 Sep Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `3`

Under isothermal conditions, two soap bubbles of radii ' $r_1$ ' and ' $r_2$ ' combine to forms a single soap bubble of radius ' $R$ '. The surface tension of soap solution is ( $\mathrm{P}=$ outside pressure)

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\frac{\mathrm{P}\left(\mathrm{R}^3+\mathrm{r}_1^3+\mathrm{r}_2^3\right)}{4\left(\mathrm{r}_1^2-\mathrm{r}_2^2+\mathrm{R}^2\right)}$
- **(B)** $\frac{\mathrm{P}\left(\mathrm{R}^2+\mathrm{r}_1^2+\mathrm{r}_2^2\right)}{4\left(\mathrm{r}_1^2-\mathrm{r}_2^2+\mathrm{R}^2\right)}$
- **(C)** $\frac{P\left(R^3-r_1^3-r_2^3\right)}{4\left(r_1^2+r_2^2-R^2\right)}$
- **(D)** $\frac{\mathrm{P}\left(\mathrm{R}^2-\mathrm{r}_1^2-\mathrm{r}_2^2\right)}{4\left(\mathrm{r}_1^3+\mathrm{r}_2^3-\mathrm{R}^3\right)}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) $\frac{P\left(R^3-r_1^3-r_2^3\right)}{4\left(r_1^2+r_2^2-R^2\right)}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Pressure inside the first bubble $=\mathrm{P}+\frac{4 \mathrm{~T}}{\mathrm{r}_1}$
Pressure inside the second bubble $=\mathrm{P}+\frac{4 \mathrm{~T}}{\mathrm{r}_2}$
Using the formula $\mathrm{PV}=\mathrm{nR} \theta \quad(\theta=$ absolute temp $)$
$\left(\mathrm{P}+\frac{4 \mathrm{~T}}{\mathrm{r}_1}\right) \cdot \frac{4 \pi}{3} \mathrm{r}_1^3=\mathrm{n}_1 \mathrm{R} \theta \quad$ ( $\mathrm{R}$ is molar gas constant)
$\left(\mathrm{P}+\frac{4 \mathrm{~T}}{\mathrm{r}_2}\right) \cdot \frac{4 \pi}{3} \mathrm{r}_2^3=\mathrm{n}_2 \mathrm{R} \theta$
and $\left(\mathrm{P}+\frac{4 \mathrm{~T}}{\mathrm{R}}\right) \cdot \frac{4 \pi}{3} \mathrm{R}^3=\left(\mathrm{n}_1+\mathrm{n}_2\right) \mathrm{R} \theta$
$\therefore\left(\mathrm{P}+\frac{4 \mathrm{~T}}{\mathrm{R}}\right) \cdot \frac{4 \pi}{3} \mathrm{R}^3=\left(\mathrm{P}+\frac{4 \mathrm{~T}}{\mathrm{r}_1}\right) \cdot \frac{4 \pi}{3} \mathrm{r}_1^3+\left(\mathrm{P}+\frac{4 \mathrm{~T}}{\mathrm{r}_2}\right) \cdot \frac{4 \pi \mathrm{r}_2^3}{3}$
$\therefore\left(\mathrm{P}+\frac{4 \mathrm{~T}}{\mathrm{R}}\right) \mathrm{R}^3=\left(\mathrm{P}+\frac{4 \mathrm{~T}}{\mathrm{r}_1}\right) \mathrm{r}_1^3+\left(\mathrm{P}+\frac{4 \mathrm{~T}}{\mathrm{r}_2}\right) \mathrm{r}_2^3$
on solving: $\mathrm{T}=\frac{\mathrm{P}\left(\mathrm{R}^3-\mathrm{r}_1^3-\mathrm{r}_2^3\right)}{4\left(\mathrm{r}_1^2+\mathrm{r}_2^2-\mathrm{R}^2\right)}$

</details>

---

### Q170. MHT CET 2021 (21 Sep Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

Two small drops of mercury each of radius ' $R$ ' coalesce to from a large single drop. The ratio of the total surface energies before and after the change is

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\sqrt{2}: 1$
- **(B)** $2^{2 / 3}: 1$
- **(C)** $2^{1 / 3}: 1$
- **(D)** $2: 1$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) $2^{1 / 3}: 1$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Total surface energy before coalesce
$$
E_1=2\left(4 \pi R^2\right) T
$$
But $\frac{4}{3} \pi \mathrm{R}^3 \times 2=\frac{4}{3} \pi \mathrm{R}^{13}$
$$
R^{\prime}=(2)^{1 / 3} R
$$
Total surface energy after coalescing
$$
\begin{aligned}
& \mathrm{E}_2=4 \pi R^{\prime 2} \mathrm{~T}=4 \pi(2)^{2 / 3} R^2 \mathrm{~T} \\
& \frac{\mathrm{E}_1}{\mathrm{E}_2}=\frac{2\left(4 \pi R^2\right) \mathrm{T}}{4 \pi(2)^{2 / 3} \mathrm{R}^2 \mathrm{~T}}=2^{1-\frac{2}{3}}=2^{1 / 3}
\end{aligned}
$$

</details>

### Q172. MHT CET 2021 (20 Sep Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

Let ' $\mathrm{R}_1$ ' and ' $\mathrm{R}_2$ ' are radii of two mercury drops. A big mercury drop is formed from then under isothermal conditions. The radius of the resultant drop is

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\sqrt{\mathrm{R}_1^2+\mathrm{R}_2^2}$
- **(B)** $\left(\mathrm{R}_1^3+\mathrm{R}_2^3\right)^{\frac{1}{3}}$
- **(C)** $\sqrt{\mathrm{R}_1^2-\mathrm{R}_2^2}$
- **(D)** $\frac{R_1+R_2}{2}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) $\left(\mathrm{R}_1^3+\mathrm{R}_2^3\right)^{\frac{1}{3}}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The volume of the bigger drop will be equal to the sum of the volumes of the smaller drops
$$
\begin{aligned}
& \frac{4}{3} \pi \mathrm{R}^3=\frac{4}{3} \pi \mathrm{R}_1^3+\frac{4}{3} \pi \mathrm{R}_2^3 \\
& \therefore \mathrm{R}=\left(\mathrm{R}_1^3+\mathrm{R}_2^3\right)^{1 / 3}
\end{aligned}
$$

</details>

### Q173. MHT CET 2021 (20 Sep Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

The force required to take away a flat circular plate of radius 2 $\mathrm{cm}$ from the surface of water is
$\left[\right.$ Surface tension of water $\left.=70 \times 10^{-3} \mathrm{Nm}^{-1}, \pi=\frac{22}{7}\right]$

**Options:**

- **(A)** $4.4 \times 10^{-4} \mathrm{~N}$
- **(B)** $8.8 \times 10^{-3} \mathrm{~N}$
- **(C)** $6.6 \times 10^{-4} \mathrm{~N}$
- **(D)** $11 \times 10^{-3} \mathrm{~N}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) $8.8 \times 10^{-3} \mathrm{~N}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The force required to take away circular plate due to surface tension
$$
\begin{aligned}
& F=2 \pi r \mathrm{~T} \\
& =2 \times \frac{22}{7} \times 2 \times 10^{-2} \times 70 \times 10^{-3} \\
& =8.8 \times 10^{-3} \mathrm{~N}
\end{aligned}
$$

</details>

### Q175. MHT CET 2021 (20 Sep Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

What should be the radius of water drop so that excess pressure inside it is $72 \mathrm{Nm}^{-2}$ ? (The surface tension of water $7.2 \times 10^{-2} \mathrm{Nm}^{-1}$ )

**Options:**

- **(A)** $1 \mathrm{~mm}$
- **(B)** $2 \mathrm{~mm}$
- **(C)** $8\mathrm{~mm}$
- **(D)** $4 \mathrm{~mm}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) $2 \mathrm{~mm}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Excess pressure in a water drop $=\frac{2 T}{R}$
$$
\begin{aligned}
& \mathrm{T}=7.2 \times 10^{-2} \mathrm{Nm}^{-2} \\
& \therefore 72=\frac{2 \times 7.2 \times 10^{-2}}{\mathrm{R}} \\
& \therefore \mathrm{R}=\frac{2 \times 7.2 \times 10^{-2}}{72}=2 \times 10^{-3} \mathrm{~m}=2 \mathrm{~mm}
\end{aligned}
$$

</details>

### Q176. MHT CET 2021 (20 Sep Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

The surface energy of a liquid drop is ' $U$ '. It splits up into 512 equal droplets. The surface energy becomes

**Options:**

- **(A)** $8 \mathrm{U}$
- **(B)** $6 \mathrm{U}$
- **(C)** $4 \mathrm{U}$
- **(D)** $2 \mathrm{U}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) $8 \mathrm{U}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

$\begin{aligned} & \frac{4}{3} \pi R^3=512 \times \frac{4}{3} \pi r^3 \\ & \therefore r=\frac{R}{8} \\ & U=4 \pi R^2 T \\ & U^{\prime}=512 \times 4 \pi r^2 T \\ & =512 \times \frac{4 \pi R^2 T}{64}=8\left(4 \pi R^2 T\right)=8 U\end{aligned}$

</details>

### Q177. MHT CET 2020 (20 Oct Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

Three liquids have same surface tension and densities $\varrho_{1}, Q_{2}$, and $\varrho_{3}\left(\varrho_{1}>\varrho_{2}>\varrho_{3}\right)$.
In three identical capillaries, rise of liquid is same. The corresponding angles of contact $\theta_{1}, \theta_{2}$ and $\theta_{3}$ are related as

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\theta_{1}>\theta_{2}>\theta_{3}$
- **(B)** $\theta_{1} < \theta_{2}>\theta_{3}$
- **(C)** $\theta_{1}>\theta_{2} < \theta_{3}$
- **(D)** $\theta_{1} < \theta_{2} < \theta_{3}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(D) $\theta_{1} < \theta_{2} < \theta_{3}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Rise of a liquid in a capillary tube is given by,
$\mathrm{h}=\frac{2 \mathrm{T} \cos \theta}{\mathrm{r} \rho \mathrm{g}}$
or $\cos \theta=h r \rho g / 2 T$
where $\theta=$ angle of contact,
$\mathrm{r}=$ radius of capillary tube
$\mathrm{T}=$ surface tension
$\rho=$ density of liquid
now given that $h$, $T$ and $r$ are constants for all three liquids,
and $\rho_{1}>\rho_{2}>\rho_{3}$,
therefore $\cos \theta_{1}>\cos \theta_{2}>\cos \theta_{3}$
or $\theta_{1} < \theta_{2} < \theta_{3}$
now as the liquid is rising in all three capillaries therefore angles of contact will be acute,
$0 \leq \theta_{1} < \theta_{2} < \theta_{3} < \frac{\pi}{2}$

</details>

### Q178. MHT CET 2020 (20 Oct Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

If ' $\mathrm{T}^{\prime}$ is the surface tension of a soap solution, then the work done in blowing a soap
bubble from diameter 'D' to diameter '2D' is

**Options:**

- **(A)** $2 \pi \mathrm{TD}^{2}$
- **(B)** $4 \pi \mathrm{TD}^{2}$
- **(C)** $8 \pi \mathrm{TD}^{2}$
- **(D)** $6 \pi \mathrm{TD}^{2}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(D) $6 \pi \mathrm{TD}^{2}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

$W=\frac{4 \pi}{4}\left(4 D^{2}-D^{2}\right) T \times 2$
$=3 \mathrm{D}^{2} \pi \mathrm{T} \times 2=6 \mathrm{D}^{2} \pi \mathrm{T}$

</details>

When a capillary tube is immersed in water vertically, water rises to a height ' $\mathrm{h}^{\prime}$ inside the tube. If the radius of another capillary tube is $\frac{1}{3}$ that of the previous, the
height to which water will rise in this tube, is

### Q181. MHT CET 2020 (19 Oct Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

Water rises upto a height 'h' in a capillary tube on the surface of the earth. The value of ' $\mathrm{h}$ ' will increase if the experimental setup is kept in $[\mathrm{g}=$ acceleration due
to gravity]

**Options:**

- **(A)** a lift going upward with a certain acceleration.
- **(B)** a lift going down with acceleration
- **(C)** accelerating train.
- **(D)** a satellite rotating close to earth.

<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) a lift going down with acceleration**

**Detailed Solution:**

(D)
$h=\frac{2 T \cos \theta}{r \rho g} \quad \therefore h \propto \frac{1}{g}$
For a lift going down with acceleration $a$, the effective value of $g$ is $g^{\prime}=g-a$ $g^{\prime}=g-a$
$\mathrm{g}^{\prime} < \mathrm{g} \quad \therefore \mathrm{h}$ will increase.

</details>

### Q182. MHT CET 2020 (19 Oct Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

A water film is formed between the two straight parallel wires, each of length 10
$\mathrm{cm}$, kept at a separation of $0 \cdot 5 \mathrm{~cm}$. Now, the separation between them is
increased by $1 \mathrm{~mm}$ without breaking the water film.
The work done for this is
(surface tension of water $=7 \cdot 2 \times 10^{-2} \mathrm{Nm}^{-1}$ )

**Options:**

- **(A)** $7 \cdot 22 \times 10^{-6} \mathrm{~J}$
- **(B)** $5 \cdot 76 \times 10^{-5} \mathrm{~J}$
- **(C)** $1 \cdot 44 \times 10^{-5} \mathrm{~J}$
- **(D)** $2 \cdot 88 \times 10^{-5} \mathrm{~J}$

<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Detailed Solution:**

(A)
Increase in the area of the film is
$\Delta \mathrm{A}=10 \mathrm{~cm} \times 0.1 \mathrm{~cm}=1 \mathrm{~cm}^{2}=10^{-4} \mathrm{~m}^{2}$
Work done $W=2 T . \Delta A$
$\begin{array}{l}
=2 \times 7.2 \times 10^{-2} \times 10^{-4} \mathrm{~J} \\
=1.44 \times 10^{-5} \mathrm{~J}
\end{array}$

</details>

### Q185. MHT CET 2020 (16 Oct Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

The work done in blowing a soap bubble of radius 'R' is ' $\mathrm{W}$ '. The work done in blowing a bubble of radius ' $2 \mathrm{R}^{\prime}$ of the same soap solution is

(B)

### Q186. MHT CET 2020 (16 Oct Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

Two small drops of mercury each of radius 'R' coalesce to form a large single drop. The ratio of the total surface energies before and after the change is

**Options:**

- **(A)** $2\frac{2}{3}:1$
- **(B)** $\sqrt{2}: 1$
- **(C)** $2\frac{1}{3}: 1$
- **(D)** $2: 1$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) $2\frac{1}{3}: 1$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The volume of mercury remains same.
$$
\begin{array}{l}
\therefore \frac{4 \pi}{3} R^{3}=2\left(\frac{4 \pi}{3} r^{3}\right) \\
\therefore R^{3}=2 r^{3} \quad \text { or } \quad r^{3}=\frac{R^{3}}{2} \\
\therefore r=\frac{R}{2^{\frac{1}{3}}}
\end{array}
$$
Initial surface energy $\mathrm{E}_{1}=4 \pi \mathrm{R}^{2} \cdot \mathrm{T}$
Final surface energy
$\mathrm{E}_{2}=4 \pi \mathrm{r}^{2} \mathrm{~T}=4 \pi\left(\frac{\mathrm{R}}{2^{\frac{1}{3}}}\right)^{2} \mathrm{~T}=4 \pi \frac{\mathrm{R}^{2}}{2^{\frac{2}{3}} \mathrm{~T}}$
$\therefore \frac{E_{1}}{E_{2}}=2^{\frac{2}{3}}$

</details>

---

### Q187. MHT CET 2020 (16 Oct Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

Water rises in a capillary tube of radius $\mathrm{r}$ upto a height 'h'. The mass of water in a capillary is 'm'. The mass of water that will rise in a capillary of radius $\frac{\mathrm{r}}{4}$ will be

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\frac{m}{4}$
- **(B)** $\frac{4}{\mathrm{~m}}$
- **(C)** 4m
- **(D)** m

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) $\frac{m}{4}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

$\mathrm{hr}=$ constant
$\therefore$ rise of water in capillary of radius $\frac{\mathrm{r}}{4}$ is $4 \mathrm{~h}$
Now, $\pi \mathrm{r}^{2} \mathrm{~h} \rho=\mathrm{m}$
$\therefore \quad \pi\left(\frac{\mathrm{r}}{4}\right)^{2} \times 4 \mathrm{~h} \rho=\frac{\mathrm{m}}{4}$

</details>

### Q188. MHT CET 2020 (15 Oct Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

Water rises to a height $3 \mathrm{~cm}$ in a capillary tube. If cross-sectional area of capillary tube is reduced to $\frac{1}{0}$ th initial area then water will rise to a height of

**Options:**

- **(A)** $9 \mathrm{~cm}$
- **(B)** $6 \mathrm{~cm}$
- **(C)** $7 \mathrm{~cm}$
- **(D)** $8 \mathrm{~cm}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) $9 \mathrm{~cm}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

$\mathrm{h}_{1} \mathrm{r}_{1}=\mathrm{h}_{2} \mathrm{r}_{2}$
$\frac{\mathrm{h}_{1}}{\mathrm{~h}_{2}}=\frac{\mathrm{r}_{2}}{\mathrm{r}_{1}}$
$\mathrm{~A} \propto \mathrm{r}^{2}$
$\sqrt{\mathrm{A}} \propto \mathrm{r}$
$\therefore \quad \mathrm{h}_{2}=\mathrm{h}_{1} \sqrt{\frac{\mathrm{A}_{1}}{\mathrm{~A}_{2}}}=3 \sqrt{9}=3 \times 3=9 \mathrm{~cm}$

</details>

### Q189. MHT CET 2020 (15 Oct Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

Due to surface tension, the excess pressure inside a smaller drop is 9 units. If 27 smaller drops combine, then the excess pressure inside the bigger drop is

**Options:**

- **(A)** $2$ units
- **(B)** $1$ unit
- **(C)** $3$ units
- **(D)** $4$ units

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) $3$ units**

**Detailed Solution:**

Given $P_{\text {in }}-P_{\text {out }}=\frac{4 T}{r}=9$ units If 27 such drop coalesee $27\left(\frac{4}{3} \pi r^{3}\right)=\frac{4}{3} \pi r_{b}^{3}$
$\therefore \quad 3 r=r_{b}$
$\therefore$ Excess pressure $\frac{4 \mathrm{~T}}{\mathrm{r}_{\mathrm{b}}}=\frac{4 \mathrm{~T}}{3 \mathrm{r}}=\frac{9}{3}=3$ unit

</details>

### Q191. MHT CET 2020 (15 Oct Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

Water rises to height $2 \cdot 2 \mathrm{~cm}$ in glass capillary tube. The height to which same water rises in another capillary having $\frac{1}{4}$ area of cross-section is

**Options:**

- **(A)** $16 \cdot 4 \mathrm{~cm}$
- **(B)** $4 \cdot 4 \mathrm{~cm}$
- **(C)** $8 \cdot 4 \mathrm{~cm}$
- **(D)** $2 \cdot 2 \mathrm{~cm}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) $4 \cdot 4 \mathrm{~cm}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

$\mathrm{h}_{1}=2.2 \mathrm{~cm}, \mathrm{~h}_{2}=?$
$\mathrm{~h}_{1} \mathrm{r}_{1}=\mathrm{h}_{2} \mathrm{r}_{2}$
$\mathrm{~h}_{1} \sqrt{\mathrm{A}_{1}}=\mathrm{h}_{2} \sqrt{\mathrm{A}_{2}}$
$\mathrm{~h}_{2}=\frac{\mathrm{h}_{1} \sqrt{\mathrm{A}_{1}}}{\sqrt{\mathrm{A}_{2}}}=2.2 \times 2=4.4 \mathrm{~cm}$

</details>

### Q192. MHT CET 2020 (14 Oct Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

A capillary tube is vertically immersed in water, water rises upto a height 'h $_{1}$ '. When
the whole arrangement is taken to a depth 'd' in a mine, the water level rises upto
height ${ }^{\prime} h_{2}$ '. The ratio $\mathrm{h}_{1} / \mathrm{h}_{2}$ is
$(\mathrm{R}=$ radius of earth $)$

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\left(1+\frac{2 \mathrm{~d}}{\mathrm{R}}\right)$
- **(B)** $\left(1-\frac{d}{R}\right)$
- **(C)** $\left(1+\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{R}}\right)$
- **(D)** $\left(1-\frac{2 d}{R}\right)$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) $\left(1-\frac{d}{R}\right)$**

**Detailed Solution:**

$\begin{aligned} & h=\frac{2 T \cos \theta}{r \rho g} \\ \therefore & h \alpha \frac{1}{g} \\ \therefore & \frac{h_{1}}{h_{2}}=\frac{g_{2}}{g_{1}}=1-\frac{d}{R} \end{aligned}$

</details>

---

### Q193. MHT CET 2020 (14 Oct Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

Let ${ }^{\prime} \mathrm{R}_{1}$ ' and ${ }^{\prime} \mathrm{R}_{2}$ ' are radii of two mercury drops. A big mercury drop is formed from
them under isothermal conditions. The radius of the resultant drop is

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\mathrm{R}=\sqrt{\mathrm{R}_{1}^{2}-\mathrm{R}_{2}^{2}}$
- **(B)** $R=\frac{R_{1}+R_{2}}{2}$
- **(C)** $\mathrm{R}=\sqrt{\mathrm{R}_{1}^{2}+\mathrm{R}_{2}^{2}}$
- **(D)** $\mathrm{R}=\left(\mathrm{R}_{1}^{3}+\mathrm{R}_{2}^{3}\right)^{\frac{1}{3}}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(D) $\mathrm{R}=\left(\mathrm{R}_{1}^{3}+\mathrm{R}_{2}^{3}\right)^{\frac{1}{3}}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The Volume of the bigger drop is equal to the sum of the volumes of the smaller drops.
$\begin{aligned}
& \frac{4}{3} \pi R^{3}=\frac{4}{3} \pi R_{1}^{3}+\frac{4}{3} \pi R_{2}^{3} \\
\therefore \quad & R=\sqrt[3]{R_{1}^{3}+R_{2}^{3}}
\end{aligned}$

</details>

---

### Q194. MHT CET 2020 (14 Oct Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

The excess pressure inside the first soap bubble of radius ' $\mathrm{R}_{1}$ ' is two times, that inside the second soap bubble of radius ' $\mathrm{R}_{2}$ '. The ratio of volumes of the first bubble to that of second bubble is

**Options:**

- **(A)** $1: 4$
- **(B)** $1: 1$
- **(C)** $1: 2$
- **(D)** $1: 8$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(D) $1: 8$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Excess pressure $\quad P=\frac{4 T}{R}$
$\therefore \frac{P_{1}}{P_{2}}=\frac{R_{2}}{R_{1}}$
$\frac{P_{1}}{P_{2}}=2$
$\frac{V_{1}}{V_{2}}=\frac{\frac{4}{3} \pi R_{1}^{2}}{\frac{4}{3} \pi R_{2}^{3}}=\frac{R_{1}^{3}}{R_{2}^{3}}=\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{3}=\frac{1}{8}$

</details>

---

### Q195. MHT CET 2020 (14 Oct Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

One thousand small water drops of equal radii combine to form a big drop. The ratio of final surface energy to the total initial surface energy is

**Options:**

- **(A)** 1:1000
- **(B)** 1:1
- **(C)** $1: 10$
- **(D)** $1: 100$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) $1: 10$**

**Detailed Solution:**

$U_{1}=1000 \times 4 \pi r_{1}^{2} \times T$
$\frac{r_{2}}{r_{1}}=(1000)^{1 / 3}=10$
$U_{2}=4 \pi r_{2}^{2} T$
$\frac{U_{2}}{U_{1}}=\frac{4 \pi r_{2}^{2} T}{1000 \times 4 \pi r_{1}^{2} \times T}=\frac{100}{1000}=\frac{1}{10}$

</details>

---

### Q196. MHT CET 2020 (13 Oct Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

small drops of liquid of same radius coalesce to form a big drop. The ratio of
the total surface energies after and before the change is

**Options:**

- **(A)** $2^{3}: 1$
- **(B)** $2^{-\frac{1}{3}}: 1$
- **(C)** $2^{-\frac{2}{3}}: 1$
- **(D)** $2^{\frac{2}{3}}: 1$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) $2^{-\frac{1}{3}}: 1$**

**Detailed Solution:**

$2 \times \frac{4}{3} \pi r^{3}=\frac{4}{3} \pi R^{3} \quad \therefore \quad R=2^{\frac{1}{3}} r$
Ratio of energies $\frac{E_{2}}{E_{1}}=\frac{4 \pi R^{2} T}{2 \times \pi r^{2} \times T}$
$\frac{E_{2}}{E_{1}}=\frac{R^{2}}{2 r^{2}}=\frac{2^{\frac{2}{3}} r^{2}}{2 r^{2}}=2^{\frac{2}{3}-1}=2^{-\frac{1}{3}}$

</details>

---

### Q197. MHT CET 2020 (13 Oct Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

The excess pressure inside a spherical drop of water is three times that of another
drop of water. The ratio of their surface area is

**Options:**

- **(A)** $3: 1$
- **(B)** $6: 1$
- **(C)** $1: 9$
- **(D)** $1: 3$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) $1: 9$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Excess pressure $p=\frac{2 T}{r}$
$\frac{p_{1}}{p_{2}}=\frac{r_{2}}{r_{1}}=3$
$\begin{array}{l}
\therefore r_{2}=3 r_{1} \\
\frac{A_{1}}{A_{2}}=\frac{\pi r_{1}^{2}}{\pi r_{2}^{2}}=\left(\frac{r_{1}}{r_{2}}\right)^{2}=\frac{1}{(3)^{2}}=\frac{1}{9}
\end{array}$

</details>

---

### Q198. MHT CET 2020 (13 Oct Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

Water rises to a height of $15 \mathrm{~mm}$ in a capillary tube having cross-sectional area 'A'.
If cross-sectional area of the tube is made ' $\frac{A^{\prime}}{3}$ then the water will rise to a height
of

**Options:**

- **(A)** $15 \sqrt{3} \times 10^{-3} \mathrm{~m}$
- **(B)** $20 \sqrt{3} \times 10^{-3} \mathrm{~m}$
- **(C)** $5 \sqrt{3} \times 10^{-3} \mathrm{~m}$
- **(D)** $10 \sqrt{3} \times 10^{-3} \mathrm{~m}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) $15 \sqrt{3} \times 10^{-3} \mathrm{~m}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

$\mathrm{h} \propto \frac{1}{\mathrm{r}}$ since $\mathrm{A}=\pi \mathrm{r}^{2}, \quad \sqrt{\mathrm{A}} \propto \mathrm{r}$
$\therefore \mathrm{h} \propto \frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathrm{A}}}$
$\therefore \frac{\mathrm{h}_{2}}{\mathrm{~h}_{1}}=\sqrt{\frac{\mathrm{A}_{1}}{\mathrm{~A}_{2}}}=\sqrt{3} \quad \because \frac{\mathrm{A}_{1}}{\mathrm{~A}_{2}}=3$
$\therefore \mathrm{h}_{2}=\sqrt{3} \mathrm{~h}_{1}=\sqrt{3} \times 15 \times 10^{-3} \mathrm{~m} \quad=15 \sqrt{3} \times 10^{-3} \mathrm{~m}$

</details>

---

### Q199. MHT CET 2020 (13 Oct Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

Water rises upto a height ' $\mathrm{h}$ ' in a capillary tube on the surface of the earth. The
value of 'h' increases, if the capillary tube apparatus is kept

**Options:**

- **(A)** in a lift going upward with acceleration.
- **(B)** on the sun.
- **(C)** on the poles.
- **(D)** in a lift going downward with acceleration (a) where a $ < \mathrm{g}$ (acceleration
due to gravity)

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(D) in a lift going downward with acceleration (a) where a $ < \mathrm{g}$ (acceleration
due to gravity)**

**Detailed Solution:**

$h=\frac{2 T \cos \theta}{r \rho g} \quad \therefore h \propto \frac{1}{g}$
In a lift going downwards with acceleration $a$, the apparent value of $g$ decreases and hence $b$ increases.

</details>

### Q200. MHT CET 2020 (12 Oct Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

Under isothermal conditions, two soap bubbles of radii ' $r_{1}$ ' and ' $r_{2}$ ' coalesce to form a big drop. The radius of the big drop is

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\left(r_{1}-r_{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}$
- **(B)** $\left(r_{1}+r_{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}$
- **(C)** $\left(r_{1}^{2}+r_{2}^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}$
- **(D)** $\left(r_{1}^{2}-r_{2}^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) $\left(r_{1}^{2}+r_{2}^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Under isothermal conditions the surface tension remains constant. If $r$ is the radius of the digger drop, then Final surface energy $=$ Initial surface energy $\therefore 8 \pi r^{2} T=8 \pi r_{1}^{2} T+8 \pi r_{2}^{2} T$
$\therefore r^{2}=r_{1}^{2}+r_{2}^{2}$
$\therefore r=\left(r_{1}^{2}+r_{2}^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}$

</details>

---

### Q201. MHT CET 2020 (12 Oct Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

When a mercury drop of radius 'R', breaks into 'n' droplets of equal size, the radius 'r' of each droplet is

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\mathrm{r}=\frac{\mathrm{R}}{\sqrt{\mathrm{n}}}$
- **(B)** $\mathrm{r}=\frac{\mathrm{R}}{\mathrm{n}}$
- **(C)** $\mathrm{r}=\frac{\mathrm{R}}{\mathrm{n}^{\frac{1}{3}}}$
- **(D)** $\mathrm{r}=\mathrm{R} \mathrm{n}^{\frac{1}{3}}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) $\mathrm{r}=\frac{\mathrm{R}}{\mathrm{n}^{\frac{1}{3}}}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Volume remains constant
$\therefore \mathrm{n} \times \frac{4}{3} \pi \mathrm{r}^{3}=\frac{4}{3} \pi \mathrm{R}^{3}$
$\therefore \mathrm{nr}^{3}=\mathrm{R}^{3}$
$\therefore \quad n^{\frac{1}{3}} r=R \quad \therefore r=\frac{R}{n^{\frac{1}{3}}}$

</details>

### Q202. MHT CET 2020 (12 Oct Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

A wire of length $10 \mathrm{~cm}$ is gently placed horizontally on the surface of water having
surface tension of $75 \times 10^{3} \mathrm{~N} / \mathrm{m}$. What force is required to just pull up the wire
from the water surface?

**Options:**

- **(A)** $15 \times 10^{-2} \mathrm{~N}$
- **(B)** $7 \cdot 5 \times 10^{-2} \mathrm{~N}$
- **(C)** $1 \cdot 5 \times 10^{-2} \mathrm{~N}$
- **(D)** $75 \times 10^{-2} \mathrm{~N}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) $1 \cdot 5 \times 10^{-2} \mathrm{~N}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

$F=2(T L)=2 \times 75 \times 10^{-3} \times 10 \times 10^{-2}$
$=150 \times 10^{-4}=1.5 \times 10^{-2} \mathrm{~N}$

</details>

### Q203. MHT CET 2020 (12 Oct Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

Two small drops of mercury each of radius 'R' coalesce to form a large single drop.
The ratio of the total surface energies before and after the change is

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\sqrt{2}: 1$
- **(B)** $2^{1 / 3}: 1$
- **(C)** $2: 1$
- **(D)** $2^{2 / 3}: 1$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) $2^{1 / 3}: 1$**

**Detailed Solution:**

$\frac{4}{3} \pi R^{3} \times 2=\frac{4}{3} \pi R^{\prime 3}$
$R^{\prime}=2^{1 / 3} R$
Total surface energy before $=2 \times 4 \pi R^{2} T \quad(T=$ surface tension $)$
Total surface energy after $=4 \pi R^{\prime 2} T$
$\therefore \quad$ Ratio $=\frac{2 \times R^{2}}{R^{\prime 2}}=\frac{2 \times R^{2}}{2^{2 / 3} R^{2}}=\frac{2}{2^{2 / 3}}=2^{1-\frac{2}{3}}=2^{1 / 3}$
$\therefore$ Ratio $=2^{1 / 3}: 1$

</details>

---

### Q204. MHT CET 2019 (02 May Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

Two capillary tubes of different diameters are dipped in water. The rise of water is

**Options:**

- **(A)** zero is both the tubes
- **(B)** same in both the tubes
- **(C)** more in the tube of larger diameter
- **(D)** more in the tube of smaller diameter

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(D) more in the tube of smaller diameter**

**Detailed Solution:**

<math><mi>h</mi><mo>=</mo><mfrac><mrow><mn>2</mn><mi>T</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">c</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">o</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">s</mi><mi>θ</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>ρ</mi><mi>R</mi><mi>g</mi></mrow></mfrac></math>  
<math><mi>h</mi><mo>∝</mo><mfrac><mrow><mn>1</mn></mrow><mrow><mi>R</mi></mrow></mfrac></math>  
∴ Rise of water in Capillary will be more in thinner tube.

</details>

### Q205. MHT CET 2019 (02 May Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

In air, a charged soap bubble of radius ‘ <math><mi>R</mi></math> ’ breaks into <math><mn>27</mn></math> small soap bubbles of equal radius ‘ <math><mi>r</mi></math> ’. Then, the ratio of mechanical force acting per unit area of big soap bubble to that of a small soap bubble is

**Options:**

- **(A)** <math><mfrac><mrow><mn>1</mn></mrow><mrow><mn>81</mn></mrow></mfrac></math>
- **(B)** <math><mfrac><mrow><mn>3</mn></mrow><mrow><mn>1</mn></mrow></mfrac></math>
- **(C)** <math><mfrac><mrow><mn>1</mn></mrow><mrow><mn>3</mn></mrow></mfrac></math>
- **(D)** <math><mfrac><mrow><mn>9</mn></mrow><mrow><mn>1</mn></mrow></mfrac></math>

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(D) <math><mfrac><mrow><mn>9</mn></mrow><mrow><mn>1</mn></mrow></mfrac></math>**

**Detailed Solution:**

<math><mo>∵</mo><mo> </mo><mi>R</mi><mo> </mo><mo>=</mo><mo> </mo><mfenced separators="|"><mrow><msup><mrow><mi>n</mi></mrow><mrow><mfrac><mrow><mn>1</mn></mrow><mrow><mn>3</mn></mrow></mfrac></mrow></msup></mrow></mfenced><mo>.</mo><mi>r</mi></math>  
<math><mi>R</mi><mo>=</mo><msup><mrow><mfenced separators="|"><mrow><mn>27</mn></mrow></mfenced></mrow><mrow><mfrac><mrow><mn>1</mn></mrow><mrow><mn>3</mn></mrow></mfrac></mrow></msup><mo>.</mo><mi>r</mi></math>  
<math><mi>R</mi><mo> </mo><mo>=</mo><mn>3</mn><mi>r</mi></math>  
<math><mo>∴</mo><mo> </mo><mfrac><mrow><mi>F</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>A</mi></mrow></mfrac><mo>∝</mo><mfrac><mrow><mn>1</mn></mrow><mrow><msup><mrow><mi>r</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn></mrow></msup></mrow></mfrac></math>  
<math><mfrac><mrow><mfenced separators="|"><mrow><mfrac><mrow><mi>F</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>A</mi></mrow></mfrac></mrow></mfenced><mi mathvariant="normal">B</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">i</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">g</mi></mrow><mrow><mfenced separators="|"><mrow><mfrac><mrow><mi>F</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>A</mi></mrow></mfrac></mrow></mfenced><mi mathvariant="normal">S</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">m</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">a</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">l</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">l</mi></mrow></mfrac><mo>=</mo><mo> </mo><mfrac><mrow><mn>1</mn></mrow><mrow><mfrac><mrow><msup><mrow><mfenced separators="|"><mrow><mn>3</mn><mi>r</mi></mrow></mfenced></mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn></mrow></msup></mrow><mrow><mfrac><mrow><mn>1</mn></mrow><mrow><msup><mrow><mi>r</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn></mrow></msup></mrow></mfrac></mrow></mfrac></mrow></mfrac><mo> </mo><mo> </mo><mo> </mo><mo> </mo><mo>=</mo><mo> </mo><mfrac><mrow><mn>1</mn></mrow><mrow><mn>9</mn></mrow></mfrac></math>

</details>
